● This is a new project with no existing code, so I'm treating it as
  architectural. The path is questions, then approaches, then a written spec,
  then a plan, and no code until you approve each stage. Say so if you want it
  classified differently.

  What I understand: You're building a supplier feed ingest service. It dedupes
  resent updates, throttles noisy suppliers, and runs on several instances. The
  spec is a first pass, and the brief says to handle gaps "the way you would on
  the job." Claude Code's transcript is being judged, so decisions should be
  explicit and justified.

  Ambiguities that need a decision:
  1. Identity: is a reservation keyed by reservationId alone or by (supplierId, 
     reservationId)?
  2. "Same details": which fields count? Does updatedAtUtc count?
  3. Stale updates: what happens when an older updatedAtUtc arrives after a
     newer one (out-of-order retries)?
  4. Response contract: status codes and body for created, updated, unchanged
     and throttled.
  5. Throttle semantics:
     - Do rejected requests count toward the window? If they do, a supplier that
       keeps hammering is never released.
     - Do duplicates and invalid requests count?
     - Is the 101st request the first one rejected?
  6. Multi-instance: throttle and dedup state must live in the shared DB.
     Instance clocks can skew, so we need to decide whose clock to use. We also
     need atomicity for races on the same reservation or the same supplier.
  7. Stats meaning:
     - Is "ingested" every accepted request, or only writes (create or update)?
     - Are duplicates counted?
     - Are the counters lifetime or windowed?
     - What does an unknown supplier return?
  8. Validation: check-out must be after check-in, price must be non-negative,
     and required fields must be present. Decide whether to enforce these, and
     how to handle non-UTC timestamps.
  9. Housekeeping: the throttle log needs cleanup, which is probably a note in
     the README rather than code.

  I'll settle the four highest-impact ones first.

✻ Baked for 35s · done Wednesday 6:43 PM

❯ regrading the question about the windows timing in a scalable service - why   
  do you think we should have different clock in each machine and accept a      
  little diff between them? i think we should be as accurate as possible with   
  our rate limiting and giving differnt clock lacks single source of truth.     
  what i had in mind is one external cache such as redis that the service could 
  talk to at scale but the assignment specifies the technologies we should use  
  without redis. how about we would interact with the existing database as the  
  external source of truth?                                                     

● You're right. I framed the app-server clock as the default when it's really a
  weaker option, and I shouldn't have. For a limit that has to be exact across
  instances, the instances shouldn't each keep their own time.

  Revised decision: the database holds both the state and the clock.
  - Window state: each admitted request writes a row to a request_log table
    (supplierId, timestamp). The window is the count of rows for that supplier
    in the last 60 seconds. This is an exact sliding window, not an approximate
    fixed-bucket counter.
  - Timestamp: the DB stamps the time inside the same statement that counts, for
    example strftime(...) in SQLite. The app never supplies "now" for
    throttling, so instance clocks don't matter.
  - Atomic check-and-insert: one statement does INSERT ... SELECT ... WHERE 
    (count in window) < 100. If it affects 0 rows, the request is throttled.
    There is no separate read then write, so two instances can't both admit
    request #100. SQLite serializes writers, so this is safe there. On SQL
    Server or LocalDB the same idea needs UPDLOCK, HOLDLOCK or an applock per
    supplier, and I'd note that in the README.
  - Throttled counter: a throttled request increments a per-supplier stats
    counter in the same transaction. Throttled requests aren't logged as window
    rows, which matches your earlier decision.

  Trade-offs to be aware of:
  - SQLite caveat: SQLite's now is read from the OS clock of the process, not
    from a separate server. With a shared SQLite file, all instances are on one
    host, so there is one clock in practice. The design still ports to a real DB
    server, which is the point of keeping time in SQL.
  - Testing: we can't fake time through an IClock. The tests will insert
    backdated request_log rows directly, which is deterministic and needs no
    sleeping.
  - Write cost: every request writes a row, and the table needs periodic cleanup
    of rows older than 60 seconds. I'd handle that with an opportunistic delete
    or a note in the README, not a background service.

  The last open question is validation, and it follows from your throttle
  answer. Invalid payloads count toward the limit, so we validate after the
  throttle check.

● User answered Claude's questions:
  ⎿  · How strict should payload validation be (it runs after the throttle 
     check)? → Required fields, checkOut > checkIn, price >= 0 (Recommended)

● That covers the open questions. I'm writing the decisions into the plan file
  now, including a few smaller defaults I picked, which you can override.

● Plan mode only lets me edit the plan file, so I'll answer your questions here.
  I'll add the README and CLAUDE.md work to the plan, and I'll write those
  files once you approve.

  Stale vs. unchanged
  - unchanged: the incoming details match what's stored, so there is nothing to
    write. This is a harmless duplicate, such as a network retry.
  - stale: the incoming updatedAtUtc is older than the stored one and the
    details differ. We deliberately discard real information because it is out
    of date. It matters more than a duplicate, because a rising stale count can
    mean the supplier sends out of order. Older with identical details counts as
    unchanged, since no information is lost.
  - Neither is "ingested": only created and updated count as ingested.

  How cleanup works
  - Correctness doesn't depend on it: the count query only looks at the last 60
    seconds. Old rows are dead weight that only affect table size and query
    speed.
  - Where it runs: on every admitted request, the same transaction runs DELETE 
    FROM request_log WHERE supplierId=@s AND tsMs < now-60s. It's indexed on
    (supplierId, tsMs), so it's cheap.
  - Why the table stays small: a supplier can have at most 100 admitted rows in
    any 60-second window. A supplier that goes quiet leaves at most about 100
    leftover rows until it next sends something. The table is therefore bounded
    by roughly 100 × the number of suppliers, with no background service.

  The SQLite clock problem
  - Not a real server clock: SQLite is an in-process library. Its now is the OS
    clock of whichever app process runs the statement. "The DB is the single
    clock" only holds because every instance shares one host. The design is
    right, but SQLite can't demonstrate it.
  - Multiple hosts: instances on different machines can't safely share a SQLite
    file over a network filesystem, because locking is unreliable. Real
    multi-host needs a server DB such as SQL Server, where SYSUTCDATETIME() is a
    genuine single clock.
  - Wall-clock jumps: SQLite offers no monotonic time. An NTP correction or a
    manual clock change on that host would shift the window.
  - Testing: time can't be faked, so tests use backdated rows.

❯ ok lets go over the plan and challenge it                                     

● I went through the plan looking for weak spots. These are ordered roughly by
  how much they could bite.

  Real design problems

  1. Unchanged-but-newer updates break the stale rule.
     - Stored: details A at t=10. Supplier sends A at t=20. We say unchanged and
       don't write, so the stored version stays t=10.
     - Supplier then sends B at t=15, a delayed retry from before the t=20
       assertion. 15 > 10, so we apply B. But the supplier had already told us
       at t=20 that A was current, so we just reverted newer data.
     - The fix is to bump updatedAtUtc on an unchanged-but-newer request. That's
       a write, which conflicts with "don't write it again". We have to pick
       one.
  2. "Throttle before validation" has a hole. To throttle by supplierId we have
     to parse the body first.
     - Malformed JSON or a missing supplierId can't be attributed, so it never
       counts.
     - A hostile client can send unlimited garbage with no cost. The plan only
       throttles attributable garbage.
     - Without auth, anyone can also send supplierId: "victim" and burn the
       victim's quota. That's a throttle-DoS on another supplier. It's fine to
       accept, but it should be listed as a named limitation.
  3. The limiter can become the load problem.
     - Every request writes to the DB: a log row, a delete, and a stats counter.
       Throttled requests write too, because throttled is a counter.
     - SQLite has one writer lock for the whole file. A supplier flooding us
       makes every other supplier queue behind its rejection writes.
     - We also need BEGIN IMMEDIATE or busy_timeout, or two instances will hit
       SQLITE_BUSY on lock upgrade. That's the main failure mode in
       multi-instance SQLite.
  4. Millisecond precision isn't guaranteed. strftime('%s','now') is whole
     seconds. We need unixepoch('subsec'), which requires SQLite 3.42 or later,
     or julianday. We should check what Microsoft.Data.Sqlite actually bundles
     before promising an exact window.
  5. A future-dated updatedAtUtc poisons a reservation. If a supplier sends year
     2099 once, every later real update is stale forever. Rejecting far-future
     timestamps needs a clock, but we have only a DB clock. It could be one more
     validation clause against the DB time.

  Smaller issues

  6. Transaction scope is undecided. If the throttle admit and the reservation
     upsert share a transaction, the write lock is held longer. If they're
     separate, a crash between them burns a quota slot. I'd keep them separate
     and accept the burnt slot.
  7. Retry-After needs the oldest row in the window, which costs an extra query
     on the throttled path.
  8. Testing "multiple instances": in tests the instances are just parallel
     connections in one process, so the same clock. That is honest, but it isn't
     proof of multi-host behavior.
  9. Scope: the brief asks for a half-page README. Ours is already past that,
     and the assumptions list is long. We should trim before submitting.

  Needs your confirmation: what "no overhead" meant

  You said we shouldn't allow "any overhead" in the rate limiting. I read that
  as accuracy: never admit request 101, no slack. I wrote it up as "zero
  tolerance for overhead," and I used the same word in the README for the
  performance cost, which is heavy. Do you mean:
  - No slack: the limit is exact, with no burst allowance. That's what I built
    the plan around.
  - No performance cost: don't add DB round trips to the hot path. That's a
    different design, and it conflicts with exactness on SQLite.

● User answered Claude's questions:
  ⎿  · What did you mean by 'no overhead at all' in the rate limiting? → 
     Exactness, no slack (Recommended)
     · When an identical reservation arrives with a NEWER updatedAtUtc, should 
     we bump the stored version? → Bump it (Recommended)
     · How should we handle a far-future updatedAtUtc, which would poison later 
     updates? → Reject if > DB now + small tolerance (Recommended)
     · How should unattributable requests (malformed JSON, no supplierId) be 
     treated? → 400, uncounted, documented limitation (Recommended)

● Those four are settled, so I'm folding them into the README and the plan.

● Updated plan
  ⎿  /plan to preview

● The four challenge-round decisions are now in README.md and the plan file, and
  no code has been written.

  Decided:
  - "No overhead": this means an exact limit with no slack above 100. The README
    now says performance is the knowingly accepted trade-off.
  - Unchanged but newer: if identical details arrive with a newer updatedAtUtc,
    we bump the stored version. That's one tiny write, and it closes the revert
    hole.
  - Future dates: updatedAtUtc more than about 5 minutes ahead of the DB clock
    gets a 400.
  - Unattributable garbage: it gets a 400 and isn't counted. This and the
    spoofed-supplierId quota burn are written up as named limitations.

  Recorded as implementation notes: BEGIN IMMEDIATE plus busy_timeout, and
  checking that the bundled SQLite supports millisecond time. The throttle admit
  and the reservation upsert go in separate transactions, and Retry-After comes
  from the oldest row in the window.

  Still open, not discussed yet:
  - The README is already past the half-page limit and needs trimming before
    submission.
  - We haven't compared architecture approaches yet (project layout, whether the
    throttle is middleware or a service). That's the next stage of the
    brainstorming process.

  Do you want to move on to approaches and the written spec, or keep challenging
  the plan?

✻ Cooked for 51s · done Wednesday 7:21 PM

❯ don't ask be brainstorm questions, i want to summrize the plan,and specify    
  each request type (unchagned, duplicate etc...).                              

● Plan summary

  A new ASP.NET Core 8, SQLite and Dapper service for supplier reservation
  updates. It has two endpoints:
  - POST /api/reservations/ingest
  - GET /api/reservations/stats/{supplierId}

  Core principles:
  - Exact limit: at most 100 requests per supplier in any rolling 60 seconds,
    with no slack.
  - Database as source of truth: the DB holds both the window state and the
    clock. A request_log table is written by one atomic check-and-insert, and
    the DB stamps the time.
  - Identity and versioning:
    - A reservation is keyed by (supplierId, reservationId).
    - updatedAtUtc is its version.
    - "Same details" means roomId, checkIn, checkOut and price.
  - Stats: lifetime counters per supplier.
  - Tests: they control time with backdated request_log rows.

  Processing order for every ingest request:
  1. Parse the body. If there's no usable supplierId, return 400 and stop.
  2. Throttle: atomically admit the request or reject it.
  3. Validate the payload.
  4. Check for a far-future updatedAtUtc.
  5. Upsert the reservation and classify the outcome.

  Request types

  "Duplicate" and "unchanged" are the same thing here. Both mean the details
  match what's stored.

  Request type: Created
  When it happens: New (supplierId, reservationId)
  Response: 201 {status:"created"}
  Counts in 60s window: Yes
  Stats counter: ingested +1
  DB writes: Reservation insert
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Request type: Updated
  When it happens: Key exists, details differ, incoming updatedAtUtc ≥ stored
  Response: 200 {status:"updated"}
  Counts in 60s window: Yes
  Stats counter: ingested +1
  DB writes: Reservation update
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Request type: Unchanged, same version
  When it happens: Identical details, same updatedAtUtc (plain duplicate, retry)
  Response: 200 {status:"unchanged"}
  Counts in 60s window: Yes
  Stats counter: unchanged +1
  DB writes: None
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Request type: Unchanged, newer version
  When it happens: Identical details, newer updatedAtUtc
  Response: 200 {status:"unchanged"}
  Counts in 60s window: Yes
  Stats counter: unchanged +1
  DB writes: Bump stored updatedAtUtc only
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Request type: Unchanged, older version
  When it happens: Identical details, older updatedAtUtc
  Response: 200 {status:"unchanged"}
  Counts in 60s window: Yes
  Stats counter: unchanged +1
  DB writes: None
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Request type: Stale
  When it happens: Details differ and incoming updatedAtUtc is older than stored
  Response: 200 {status:"stale"}, stored data kept
  Counts in 60s window: Yes
  Stats counter: stale +1
  DB writes: None
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Request type: Throttled
  When it happens: Supplier already has 100 admitted requests in the last 60s
  Response: 429 plus Retry-After
  Counts in 60s window: No
  Stats counter: throttled +1
  DB writes: Counter only
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Request type: Invalid
  When it happens: Missing field, checkOut ≤ checkIn, price < 0, or updatedAtUtc

  more than about 5 minutes ahead of DB time
  Response: 400 with message
  Counts in 60s window: Yes
  Stats counter: None
  DB writes: None
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Request type: Unattributable
  When it happens: Malformed JSON or no usable supplierId
  Response: 400
  Counts in 60s window: No
  Stats counter: None
  DB writes: None
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Request type: Server error
  When it happens: Unexpected failure after admission
  Response: 500
  Counts in 60s window: Yes (the slot is burnt)
  Stats counter: None
  DB writes: None

  Details worth remembering:
  - Equal versions: equal updatedAtUtc with different details is treated as an
    update, not stale.
  - Throttle first: the throttle runs before validation, which is why invalid
    requests consume quota.
  - Window membership: only admitted requests occupy the window. A throttled
    request doesn't extend the block, so the supplier is released as soon as old
    rows age out.

  Stats endpoint

  GET /api/reservations/stats/{supplierId} returns {supplierId, ingested, 
  throttled, unchanged, stale} as lifetime counters. ingested is created plus
  updated. An unknown supplier returns 200 with zeros.

  Known limitations (in the README)

  - SQLite's clock is the host OS clock, so single-clock correctness holds only
    on one host.
  - SQLite has a single writer lock, so a flooding supplier can slow others.
  - Unattributable garbage and spoofed supplierId values aren't protected,
    because there's no auth or per-IP limit.
  - Redis would be the better window store.

  The plan file and README both reflect all of this. The remaining steps are the
  written spec, the implementation plan, then TDD, whenever you want to start
  them.

✻ Cooked for 16s · done Wednesday 7:22 PM

※ recap: We're planning a supplier feed ingest service with dedup and exact per-supplier throttling, and the decisions and request-type table are now settled in the plan file and README. Next, say go and I'll write the spec for your review before any code. (disable recaps in /config)

❯ introducing stale type for a specific scenerio where old messages are coming  
  in a delay is weird, does ingested in stats repressents the sum of the other  
  categories?                                                                   

● No. Today ingested is not the sum of the other categories. It means data
  written (created + updated). unchanged and stale are separate buckets next to
  it, so the counters don't add up to anything meaningful. The spec's wording
  ("ingested vs throttled") suggests two headline numbers, and our four counters
  blur that.

  Your point on stale is fair. From the data's perspective, stale and unchanged
  are the same outcome: the request was accepted and nothing was written. The
  only difference is why. The reasons are:
  - A duplicate: we already have exactly this.
  - An out-of-date version: we have something newer.

  A separate stats counter for a rare reordering case is more surface than the
  spec asks for.

  What I'd change:
  - Stats: three categories, mutually exclusive, so they partition every
    attributable request that wasn't invalid.
    - ingested: created + updated (data written).
    - ignored: no-ops, meaning duplicates, plus stale versions and bumps.
    - throttled: rejected with 429.
  - Response body: keep stale as a distinct status value for the supplier, but
    not as a stats counter. If the supplier sent different details and we
    discarded them, answering unchanged would mislead it. The response should
    say its data wasn't applied, while stats lump the no-ops together.
  - Invalid requests (400): they stay out of stats, so the three counters don't
    equal total traffic. I'd document that explicitly in the README rather than
    add a fourth counter.

  Net effect: the response statuses stay created, updated, unchanged and stale.
  The stats shape becomes {supplierId, ingested, ignored, throttled}. You get
  one fewer concept in stats, and the counters add up to the total of valid
  requests the service handled.

  Say the word and I'll update the plan file and README. If you'd rather drop
  stale from the response too, the cost is that a supplier can't tell its
  out-of-order update was discarded.

❯ that's better stats, but the reponse should match the stat                    

● That's cleaner. One vocabulary for responses and stats, so a supplier's
  response maps straight onto the counter it increments.

  Response contract (status equals the stats counter):

  ┌───────────────────┬──────────────┬─────────────────────────┬───────────┐
  │      Outcome      │     HTTP     │          Body           │  Stats    │
  │                   │              │                         │  counter  │
  ├───────────────────┼──────────────┼─────────────────────────┼───────────┤
  │ New reservation   │ 201          │ {status:"ingested",     │ ingested  │
  │                   │              │ detail:"created"}       │           │
  ├───────────────────┼──────────────┼─────────────────────────┼───────────┤
  │ Details changed   │ 200          │ {status:"ingested",     │ ingested  │
  │                   │              │ detail:"updated"}       │           │
  ├───────────────────┼──────────────┼─────────────────────────┼───────────┤
  │ Identical details │ 200          │ {status:"ignored",      │ ignored   │
  │                   │              │ detail:"duplicate"}     │           │
  ├───────────────────┼──────────────┼─────────────────────────┼───────────┤
  │ Older version     │              │ {status:"ignored",      │           │
  │ with different    │ 200          │ detail:"stale"}         │ ignored   │
  │ details           │              │                         │           │
  ├───────────────────┼──────────────┼─────────────────────────┼───────────┤
  │ Over the limit    │ 429 +        │ {status:"throttled",    │ throttled │
  │                   │ Retry-After  │ retryAfterSeconds}      │           │
  ├───────────────────┼──────────────┼─────────────────────────┼───────────┤
  │ Invalid or        │ 400          │ error message           │ none      │
  │ unattributable    │              │                         │           │
  └───────────────────┴──────────────┴─────────────────────────┴───────────┘

  detail is only an explanatory field. It's not counted anywhere and not
  required for the stats to add up. I'll apply this to the plan and README.

          0`; timestamps normalized to UTC. No room-overlap/double-bookin
          g checks.
      19  - `request_log` cleanup: on each admitted request, delete that 
          supplier's rows older than 60s (same transaction). Bounded at ~
          100 rows per supplier, no background service.
      20  - **SQLite caveat:** SQLite has no server clock; its "now" is t
          he OS clock of the app process. "DB as single clock" holds only
           because all instances share one host. Multi-host needs a serve
          r DB (e.g. SQL Server `SYSUTCDATETIME()` with `UPDLOCK, HOLDLOC
          K`). SQLite has no monotonic time, so host clock jumps shift th
          e window. Tests control time by inserting backdated rows. SQLit
          e also has a single writer lock: every request writes (log row,
           cleanup, counters; throttled requests too), so a flooding supp
          lier can slow others. Use `BEGIN IMMEDIATE` + `busy_timeout` to
           avoid `SQLITE_BUSY` between instances, and check the bundled S
          QLite supports ms precision (`unixepoch('subsec')`, 3.42+).

✻ Brewed for 34s · done Wednesday 7:38 PM

❯ expain here the window mechanism, how its stored in the db and how cleanup    
  works                                                                         

● The code isn't written yet, so the SQL below is illustrative.

  How the window works

  It's a sliding log. Every admitted request is its own row, and the window is
  simply "rows for this supplier from the last 60 seconds."

  Storage
  - request_log:
    - Columns are supplierId and tsMs (milliseconds, stamped by the DB).
    - There's an index on (supplierId, tsMs).
    - Throttled requests never get a row.
  - supplier_stats: one row per supplier with the ingested, ignored and
    throttled counters. It's separate because request_log gets pruned and can't
    be used for lifetime counts.

  Admit or reject, in one statement:
  WITH now AS (SELECT CAST(unixepoch('subsec') * 1000 AS INTEGER) AS t)
  INSERT INTO request_log (supplierId, tsMs)
  SELECT @supplier, t FROM now
  WHERE (SELECT COUNT(*) FROM request_log
         WHERE supplierId = @supplier AND tsMs > t - 60000) < 100;
  - Admitted: 1 row affected.
  - Throttled: 0 rows affected. Then we increment throttled, and Retry-After is
    the oldest in-window row's tsMs + 60000 - now.
  - Why it's exact: the count and the insert are one statement, run inside BEGIN
    IMMEDIATE. SQLite allows one writer at a time, so two instances can't both
    see 99 and both admit. The time comes from the DB inside the statement,
    never from the app.
  - Why a sliding log: a fixed-bucket counter lets a supplier send 100 at the
    end of one minute and 100 at the start of the next. A sliding log doesn't.

  Example
  - 100 requests are admitted between 10:00:00 and 10:00:10.
  - A request at 10:00:30 counts 100 rows, so it gets 429 and Retry-After is
    about 30s (until the oldest row ages out at 10:01:00).
  - Throttled requests add no rows, so hammering doesn't extend the block.
  - At 10:01:00.001 the first row falls outside the window (tsMs > now - 60000
    is false), so the next request is admitted.

  How cleanup works

  Cleanup only keeps the table small. It is not what makes the limit correct.

  - When: on each admitted request, in the same transaction after the insert:
  DELETE FROM request_log
  WHERE supplierId = @supplier AND tsMs <= @now - 60000;
  - Safe: it only removes rows already outside the window, which the count never
    looks at. Even if it never ran, the count would stay correct and only the
    table would grow.
  - Cheap: it's an indexed range delete, usually removing about one row per call
    at steady state.
  - Bounded: a supplier can have at most 100 admitted rows per window, so the
    table is about 100 rows × number of suppliers, with no background job.
  - Quiet suppliers: their rows linger, up to 100 each, until they next send an
    admitted request. This is harmless because they're outside the window and
    never counted.
  - One caveat: with no auth, someone can send one-off fake supplierId values,
    and each leaves up to its rows behind forever. That's the same
    unauthenticated-spoofing limitation already in plan.md. A periodic global
    purge would fix it if it mattered.

✻ Baked for 17s · done Wednesday 7:42 PM

❯ so on every call you also do cleanup? sounds inefficient, its also happens    
  from our service right? so in scale it would be even more redundant           
  trnsactions                                                                   

● Yes, you're right. As planned, every admitted request also runs a DELETE,
  issued by whichever instance handled it.

  - Per-request cost: it's an extra statement inside the write transaction on
    every request. SQLite has a single writer lock, so that extra work makes
    every other request wait longer.
  - Redundancy at scale: N instances all delete the same rows for the same
    supplier. Each delete is idempotent but still costs lock time.
  - Cleanup isn't needed that often. The count query only reads rows inside the
    60-second window through the index. Old rows sitting in the table don't slow
    it down. So per-request cleanup buys nothing over a periodic one.

  Better design: a periodic global cleanup, not per request.
  - Each instance runs a BackgroundService roughly every minute.
  - Each tick it executes one statement: DELETE FROM request_log WHERE tsMs <= 
    now - 60000, where now is the DB clock.
  - It's idempotent, so overlapping instances are safe. At worst you get a few
    redundant no-op deletes a minute, not one per request. If that still bothers
    you, a maintenance row with a compare-and-set on lastCleanupMs would let
    only one instance win each interval, but I'd skip that as over-engineering.
  - It also fixes the earlier caveat. Rows from quiet or spoofed suppliers get
    cleaned too, because the delete is global rather than tied to that supplier
    sending again.
  - Size stays bounded, at about 100 to 200 rows per supplier (the window plus
    up to one tick of aged rows).

  Cost: my earlier "no background service" claim goes away. It's about 15 lines,
  and this is a deliberate trade for removing the per-request write
  amplification.

  I'm updating plan.md accordingly.

✻ Cooked for 26s · done Wednesday 7:49 PM

❯ that is better but we should have an external service to do it and not every  
  service by its own, maybe there is a mechanism of sql that allows ttl for     
  insertions? if not, please add to readme this disagreement and that if we had 
  more time we would change the job in the service to external cleaner cron     
  job                                                                           

● SQLite has no TTL mechanism. Here's what I know about the alternatives:

  - SQLite: it has no row expiry and no event scheduler. It's an in-process
    library with no daemon to run anything on a timer.
  - SQL Server and LocalDB: no native per-row TTL either. Scheduled cleanup
    there means SQL Server Agent, which LocalDB doesn't have.
  - Other engines: MySQL has CREATE EVENT and Postgres uses pg_cron or partition
    dropping. Those are DB-side schedulers rather than true TTL, and they
    aren't available on SQLite anyway.
  - Triggers: an AFTER INSERT trigger could delete old rows, but it fires per
    insert, so it's the same per-request cost moved into the DB. No gain.

  So for the stack the assignment fixes, the in-service job stays, and the
  external cleaner goes in the README as a disagreement and a future change. I'm
  editing both docs.

      16 +- Claude proposed deleting old window rows on every request, th
         +en a cleanup job inside each service instance. I disagreed with
         + both: per-request deletes add write load on every call, and N 
         +instances each running their own cleaner is redundant. SQLite h
         +as no row TTL or scheduler, so the in-service job stays only be
         +cause the fixed stack leaves no alternative.

         +dds lock time to SQLite's single writer and is redundant across
         + instances. Correctness never depends on it (the count query on
         +ly reads in-window rows via the `(supplierId, tsMs)` index, so 
         +old rows don't slow it). Idempotent, so overlapping instances a
         +re harmless. No TTL option exists on SQLite (no row expiry, no 
         +event scheduler; a trigger would just be per-insert cost inside
         + the DB), so the in-service job is a compromise; the preferred 
         +design is a single external cleaner (cron job / DB-side schedul
         +er) — see README. Also cleans rows from quiet/spoofed suppliers
         +. Table stays ~100-200 rows per supplier.                      
      61  
      62  ## Assumptions
      63  1. Supplier identity is the `supplierId` in the body (no auth).

✻ Cogitated for 21s · done Wednesday 7:51 PM

❯ ok, so lets go through in service logic once a message is sent, show me the   
  high level algorithm                                                          

● Here is the flow for one POST /api/reservations/ingest. The SQL is the plan,
  not written yet.

  INGEST(request)

  1. PARSE
     - Body isn't valid JSON, or supplierId is missing/blank
         -> 400. Nothing counted, no DB access.

  2. THROTTLE   (transaction 1, BEGIN IMMEDIATE)
     - Atomic insert into request_log, DB-stamped time, only if
       count(supplier rows in last 60s) < 100
     - 0 rows inserted (over limit):
         increment supplier_stats.throttled
         retryAfter = oldest in-window row + 60s - DB now
         commit -> 429 {status:"throttled"} + Retry-After
     - 1 row inserted: commit (slot is now consumed, even if later steps fail)

  3. VALIDATE   (in memory, no DB)
     - Required fields present
     - checkOut > checkIn
     - price >= 0
     - Normalize timestamps to UTC
     - Failure -> 400 with message (the request still counts toward the limit)

  4. APPLY      (transaction 2, BEGIN IMMEDIATE)
     - If updatedAtUtc > DB now + ~5 min -> rollback, 400
     - Load existing row by (supplierId, reservationId)

     a) No existing row:
          INSERT reservation; ingested++
          -> 201 {status:"ingested", detail:"created"}

     b) Existing row, details identical (roomId, checkIn, checkOut, price):
          if incoming.updatedAtUtc > stored.updatedAtUtc:
              UPDATE stored.updatedAtUtc only
          ignored++
          -> 200 {status:"ignored", detail:"duplicate"}

     c) Existing row, details differ, incoming.updatedAtUtc < stored:
          ignored++
          -> 200 {status:"ignored", detail:"outofdate"}

     d) Existing row, details differ, incoming.updatedAtUtc >= stored:
          UPDATE all fields; ingested++
          -> 200 {status:"ingested", detail:"updated"}

     - Commit. Any unexpected exception -> rollback, 500
       (the quota slot from step 2 stays burnt).

  Why it's safe across instances
  - SQLite: BEGIN IMMEDIATE takes the write lock before the first read, so
    "load, compare, write" can't interleave with another instance. The unique
    index on (supplierId, reservationId) is a backstop. On a server DB we'd use
    a conditional UPDATE ... WHERE updatedAtUtc <= @new or a stricter isolation
    level instead.
  - Counters and writes: the stats counters are incremented in the same
    transaction as the reservation write. A counter never claims an ingested
    that didn't commit.

  Where the counters land

  ┌──────────────────────────┬────────────┬───────────┐
  │           Path           │ Window row │  Counter  │
  ├──────────────────────────┼────────────┼───────────┤
  │ Throttled                │ no         │ throttled │
  ├──────────────────────────┼────────────┼───────────┤
  │ Invalid (400)            │ yes        │ none      │
  ├──────────────────────────┼────────────┼───────────┤
  │ Created or updated       │ yes        │ ingested  │
  ├──────────────────────────┼────────────┼───────────┤
  │ Duplicate or out of date │ yes        │ ignored   │
  └──────────────────────────┴────────────┴───────────┘

  Stats endpoint: GET /api/reservations/stats/{supplierId} reads that supplier's
  supplier_stats row and returns {supplierId, ingested, ignored, throttled}. It
  returns zeros with 200 if there's no row.

  One thing I haven't decided: steps 2 and 4 are two transactions, so each
  request takes the write lock twice. The alternative is one transaction for
  both, which would lengthen each lock hold but halve the number of lock
  acquisitions. I'd keep them separate for now, as in plan.md.

❯ explain chunk 0                                                               

● Noted that CLAUDE.md was edited, and I'll go from its current state.

  Chunk 0: a short spike to check the design's assumptions

  Purpose: four design decisions rest on assumptions about SQLite and
  Microsoft.Data.Sqlite that I haven't verified. If one is wrong, it changes the
  design, so it's cheaper to find out before building on it. Nothing from this
  chunk is kept except the answers, which go into plan.md. I haven't run
  anything yet.

  The four questions

  1. Does the bundled SQLite give millisecond time?
     - The window uses unixepoch('subsec'), which needs SQLite 3.42 or later.
       Which version we get depends on the Microsoft.Data.Sqlite package
       version.
     - If it only returns whole seconds, timestamps appear up to 1s older than
       they are. Rows would age out early and we could admit request 101
       slightly too soon. That breaks the "exact" principle.
     - Fallback: julianday('now') converted to milliseconds. It's a float but
       precise enough.
  2. Can we get BEGIN IMMEDIATE through Dapper?
     - Microsoft.Data.Sqlite opens plain deferred transactions by default.
     - This matters for the reservation step (transaction 2), which reads the
       existing row and then writes. In a deferred transaction, a
       read-then-write can fail when upgrading the lock, and busy_timeout
       doesn't help in that case. Two instances could hit SQLITE_BUSY.
     - The throttle statement is a single INSERT ... SELECT. It takes the write
       lock itself, so it's less exposed.
     - We need to find out how to start an immediate transaction cleanly.
       Possibilities are executing a raw BEGIN IMMEDIATE, or a newer provider
       overload. I believe one exists but haven't confirmed it.
     - Fallback if it's awkward: make the reservation step one INSERT ... ON 
       CONFLICT DO UPDATE ... WHERE statement. That removes the read-then-write
       entirely.
  3. WAL and busy timeout settings
     - We need PRAGMA journal_mode=WAL, which is persisted in the DB file.
     - busy_timeout is set per connection. I believe the connection string's
       Default Timeout maps to it, but that needs checking.
     - We also need to confirm that connection pooling doesn't lose these
       settings.
  4. Does the atomic admit hold under real contention? This is the key test.
     - A small throwaway program fires 150 admit statements from 8 threads, each
       on its own connection, against one DB file.
     - Pass criteria: exactly 100 admitted, 50 rejected, and no SQLITE_BUSY
       exceptions surfacing.
     - It also checks that millisecond timestamps behave as expected.

  Output: a short "SQLite facts" note in plan.md with the confirmed package
  version and the exact settings and transaction approach to use. The spike code
  is deleted and never committed. If question 1 or 2 fails, we adjust the
  design before chunk 1.

  Cost: roughly half an hour. It needs your nod before I start, since it adds
  packages and runs code. I'd put the throwaway code outside the repo so it
  can't leak into the submission.

      56  - Reservation upsert: unique index on `(supplierId, reservation
          Id)`; conditional `UPDATE ... WHERE updatedAtUtc <= @new AND de
          tails differ`, so concurrent writers can't regress data.
      57  - Throttle admit and reservation upsert are separate transactio
          ns (a crash between burns a quota slot; accepted).
      58  - `Retry-After` comes from the oldest row in the window.
      59 -- Use `BEGIN IMMEDIATE` + `busy_timeout` to avoid `SQLITE_BUSY`
         - between instances. Verify the bundled SQLite supports ms preci
         -sion (`unixepoch('subsec')`, 3.42+).                           
      59 +- Avoid `SQLITE_BUSY` between instances with immediate transact
         +ions, WAL, and a finite `Default Timeout` (see "SQLite facts" b
         +elow).                                                         
      60  - Cleanup: a `BackgroundService` per instance, roughly once a m
          inute, runs one global `DELETE FROM request_log WHERE tsMs <= n
          ow - 60000` (DB clock). Not per request: a per-request delete a
          dds lock time to SQLite's single writer and is redundant across
           instances. Correctness never depends on it (the count query on
          ly reads in-window rows via the `(supplierId, tsMs)` index, so 
          old rows don't slow it). Idempotent, so overlapping instances a
          re harmless. No TTL option exists on SQLite (no row expiry, no 
          event scheduler; a trigger would just be per-insert cost inside
           the DB), so the in-service job is a compromise; the preferred 
          design is a single external cleaner (cron job / DB-side schedul
          er) — see README. Also cleans rows from quiet/spoofed suppliers
          . Table stays ~100-200 rows per supplier.

       90  - Concurrency: parallel requests on separate connections never

       91  - Stats counters match outcomes. Validation 400 cases includin
           g far-future `updatedAtUtc`.

       93 +## SQLite facts (chunk 0 results)                             
       94 +Measured with a throwaway console app (outside the repo, disca
          +rded) on `Microsoft.Data.Sqlite 8.0.31` + `Dapper 2.1.89`, bun
          +dled SQLite **3.53.3** (`SQLitePCLRaw.bundle_e_sqlite3 2.1.12`
          +).                                                            
       95 +1. **Millisecond time works.** `unixepoch('subsec')` is availa
          +ble; `cast(unixepoch('subsec')*1000 as integer)` gives ms. `ju
          +lianday` also works but isn't needed. The window design stands
          +.                                                             
       96 +2. **`BeginTransaction()` is already immediate.** With 100 con
          +current read-sleep-write transactions on separate connections 
          +under WAL: default `BeginTransaction()` -> 0 errors, no lost u
          +pdates; raw `BEGIN IMMEDIATE` -> 0 errors; `BeginTransaction(d
          +eferred: true)` -> 8/100 failed with `SQLite Error 5: database
          + is locked`. So the risk I raised is real, but only for explic
          +it deferred transactions. Use the default `BeginTransaction()`
          +; no raw `BEGIN IMMEDIATE` needed. (Corrects my earlier assump
          +tion that it defaulted to deferred.)                          
       97 +3. **Waiting on locks is controlled by `Default Timeout` (seco
          +nds, the command timeout), not by `PRAGMA busy_timeout`** (whi
          +ch reads 0; the provider retries itself). A writer blocked by 
          +a held lock failed after ~1.1s with `Default Timeout=1` and wa
          +ited ~1.6s then succeeded with `30`. `Default Timeout=0` means
          + no limit (waited indefinitely), so set an explicit finite val
          +ue (e.g. 5-30s).                                              
       98 +4. **WAL:** `PRAGMA journal_mode=WAL` persists in the DB file 
          +only once the file has been written (setting it on an empty ne
          +w file reverted to `delete`). Set it in the same init step tha
          +t creates the schema.                                         
       99 +5. **The atomic admit holds under contention:** 150 admit stat
          +ements from 8 threads, each on its own connection, against one
          + file: exactly 100 admitted, 50 rejected, 0 errors, 100 rows i
          +n the log — both as a single autocommit statement and inside a
          +n explicit transaction.

● Chunk 0 is done. The design holds as written, and the spike code is deleted.
  It was in the job temp directory, never in the repo. The findings are in a new
  "SQLite facts" section in plan.md, and chunk 0 is marked done there.

  Results (Microsoft.Data.Sqlite 8.0.31, Dapper 2.1.89, SQLite 3.53.3):
  1. Millisecond time works. unixepoch('subsec') is available, so the
     exact-window design stands.
  2. BeginTransaction() is already immediate by default. My earlier worry that 
     it defaults to deferred was wrong.
     - I ran 100 concurrent read-sleep-write transactions on separate
       connections.
     - Default BeginTransaction() and raw BEGIN IMMEDIATE both gave 0 errors and
       no lost updates.
     - BeginTransaction(deferred: true) failed 8 times out of 100 with "database
       is locked".
     - So I'll use the default and skip the raw BEGIN IMMEDIATE.
  3. Lock waiting is governed by Default Timeout, not PRAGMA busy_timeout.
     - busy_timeout reads 0.
     - Default Timeout=1 failed after about 1.1s when a lock was held, and 30
       waited about 1.6s and succeeded.
     - Default Timeout=0 means no limit, so we'll set an explicit finite value.
  4. WAL only persists after the file has been written. Setting it on an empty
     new file reverted to the default mode, so it goes in the same init step
     that creates the schema.
  5. The atomic admit holds under contention. 150 requests from 8 connections
     gave exactly 100 admitted, 50 rejected and 0 errors, both as a single
     statement and inside a transaction.

  Not covered: separate OS processes (threads in one process stood in for them,
  which I expect behaves the same), the throttled-counter increment and the
  reservation upsert. Chunks 4, 5 and 8 cover those.

  Repo state: plan.md and CLAUDE.md have uncommitted changes, and .gitignore is
  untracked. The CLAUDE.md change is yours. I haven't committed anything.

  Chunk 2: schema and connection factory

  Goal: a SQLite database with the three tables the design needs, and one place
  that opens connections with the settings the spike validated. No business
  logic yet.

  What I'd add (all under src/SupplierFeed.Api/Data/):
  - DatabaseOptions and SqliteConnectionFactory:
    - The connection string comes from config (ConnectionStrings:Default,
      defaulting to a local supplierfeed.db).
    - The factory forces a finite Default Timeout (15s), because the spike
      showed 0 means wait forever.
    - Open() returns an open connection, and Dapper uses it directly.
  - DatabaseInitializer: idempotent CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS, run once at
    startup before the app serves traffic. PRAGMA journal_mode=WAL is set in the
    same step as the schema, since the spike showed it only sticks once the
    file has been written.

  Schema:

  ┌────────────────┬───────────────────────────┬────────────────────────────┐
  │     Table      │          Columns          │           Notes            │
  ├────────────────┼───────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────┤
  │                │ supplierId,               │ Primary key (supplierId,   │
  │ reservations   │ reservationId, roomId,    │ reservationId), which is   │
  │                │ checkInMs, checkOutMs,    │ the identity decision.     │
  │                │ price, updatedAtMs        │                            │
  ├────────────────┼───────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────┤
  │                │                           │ Index on (supplierId,      │
  │ request_log    │ supplierId, tsMs          │ tsMs) for the window       │
  │                │                           │ count.                     │
  ├────────────────┼───────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────┤
  │                │ supplierId (PK),          │                            │
  │ supplier_stats │ ingested, ignored,        │ Counters default to 0.     │
  │                │ throttled                 │                            │
  └────────────────┴───────────────────────────┴────────────────────────────┘

  Two storage decisions to confirm:
  1. Timestamps as integer milliseconds since the epoch (UTC), not ISO strings.
     That's one unambiguous format, and it matches the DB clock's
     unixepoch('subsec') for the updatedAtUtc far-future check and for the
     window.
  2. Price stored via the provider's default decimal mapping, which is TEXT,
     with equality compared in C# as decimal, so 450.0 equals 450.00. The
     alternative is integer cents, which assumes two decimals and would silently
     mishandle something like 450.005. I recommend the decimal-as-TEXT option.
     The cost is that price can't be compared inside SQL, but we load the
     existing row anyway.

✻ Sautéed for 32s · done Wednesday 8:51 PM

❯ the tests are good, the first desicion too but explain the second one         

● Decision 2: how to store price

  SQLite has no real decimal type, so there are three options. It matters
  because the dedup rule is "are the details identical?", and price is one of
  those details.

  Option A: floating point (REAL). This is what SQLite would do with a numeric
  column.
  - Binary floats can't represent most decimals exactly (0.1 + 0.2 isn't 0.3).
  - The risk for us is two different prices collapsing into one stored value.
    We'd then call a real change a duplicate and silently skip the update. It's
    a rare edge case, but it's the failure mode we most want to avoid. Not
    recommended for money.

  Option B: integer cents. The value is stored as 45000 for 450.00.
  - It's exact, but it bakes in a scale of two decimals, and the spec gives us
    no currency.
  - A supplier sending 450.005, or a currency with 0 or 3 decimals, forces a
    choice. We either reject it with a 400 (a new rule nobody asked for) or
    round it, which silently changes the supplier's data.

  Option C: exact decimal stored as text (my recommendation).
  - Microsoft.Data.Sqlite writes a C# decimal as TEXT, such as "450.00". The
    JSON parser reads the number straight into decimal, not through a double. So
    whatever precision the supplier sent round-trips exactly.
  - We compare as decimal values in C# after loading the row, not as text in
    SQL. That makes 450.0 and 450.00 equal, so a supplier changing only its
    formatting isn't treated as a change. Comparing the raw text in SQL would
    get that wrong, and we never need to.

  Two catches:
  - Column type: the column must be declared TEXT. If it were declared NUMERIC,
    SQLite would convert the text to a float and quietly bring Option A's
    problem back. I'll add a test that round-trips a high-precision price to
    guard against it.
  - No numeric queries: we can't sort or sum price in SQL without a cast.
    Nothing in the spec needs that. A real reporting feature would pay that cost
    later.

  So Option C gives exact equality for dedup, makes no assumption about currency
  scale, and rejects nothing the spec allows. Do you want to go with it? If so,
  I'll start chunk 2 with the TEXT guard test included.

● CLAUDE.md is as you left it. Here is chunk 3, described before I implement it.

  Chunk 3: parsing, validation and models (pure code, no DB)

  Goal: turn a raw request body into one of three outcomes, deciding the
  "unattributable" split from our throttle-first design. It also defines the
  response vocabulary. No endpoint or DB access yet.

  Two-stage parsing. The throttle runs before validation, so we must read
  supplierId first and tolerantly:

  ┌─────────────────────────────────┬─────────────────────────┬─────────────┐
  │                                 │                         │   Counts    │
  │              Body               │         Result          │ toward the  │
  │                                 │                         │   limit?    │
  ├─────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────┼─────────────┤
  │ Not valid JSON, not a JSON      │                         │             │
  │ object, or supplierId missing,  │ Unattributable          │ No          │
  │ blank or not a string           │                         │             │
  ├─────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────┼─────────────┤
  │ supplierId is fine but other    │ Invalid, carrying       │             │
  │ fields are wrong, such as       │ supplierId plus error   │ Yes         │
  │ price: "abc" or a missing       │ messages                │             │
  │ roomId                          │                         │             │
  ├─────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────┼─────────────┤
  │ Everything valid                │ Valid, carrying the     │ Yes         │
  │                                 │ normalized request      │             │
  └─────────────────────────────────┴─────────────────────────┴─────────────┘

  Validation rules. They collect all errors in one pass, not just the first:
  - reservationId and roomId must be non-blank.
  - checkIn, checkOut, price and updatedAtUtc must be present.
  - price >= 0; zero is allowed.
  - checkOut > checkIn.
  - The far-future updatedAtUtc check is not here. It needs the DB clock, so it
    belongs in chunk 5.

  Output model. The valid result is a ValidatedIngest holding timestamps already
  as UTC integer milliseconds and price as decimal, exactly what the stores
  need, so later chunks convert nothing.

  Response vocabulary.
  - IngestStatus: ingested, ignored, throttled.
  - IngestDetail: created, updated, duplicate, outofdate.
  - Both serialize as lowercase strings. outofdate needs a small custom naming
    policy, because the built-in ones would give outOfDate.
  - The 400 body is { "errors": [ ... ] }.

  Files: src/SupplierFeed.Api/Domain/ (parser, ValidatedIngest, the two enums,
  response models).

  Tests, written first: unit tests only.
  - Unattributable: one case each for malformed JSON, a JSON array, a missing
    supplierId, a blank supplierId, and a numeric supplierId.
  - Attributable but invalid: price: "abc" keeps the supplierId.
  - Each validation rule: one failing case per rule, plus multiple errors
    reported together.
  - Timestamps: an +02:00 offset is converted to UTC.
  - Serialization: outofdate comes out lowercase.

  Two defaults I'd like you to confirm:
  1. Timestamps with no offset (for example 2026-08-01T00:00:00 with no Z) are
     treated as UTC. The fields are documented as UTC. The default .NET parse
     would silently use the server's local time zone, which makes behavior
     machine-dependent. The strict alternative is a 400 for them.
  2. supplierId is compared exactly, with no trimming or case folding, so S1 and
     s1 are different suppliers. Normalizing wouldn't close the real hole,
     because without auth a supplier can invent new IDs anyway, which is already
     a named limitation.

  Do you want to go ahead with these defaults, or change either one?

※ recap: We're building the supplier feed throttle and dedup service chunk by chunk; chunks 1 and 2 are pushed, and I've described chunk 3 (parsing and validation). Next I need your go-ahead on its two defaults, offset-less timestamps as UTC and exact supplierId matching.

● Let me verify this by running it, so I'm not explaining from memory. I'll use

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

● It's confirmed by running it. The program and its folder are deleted.

  What happens to "2026-08-01T00:00:00" with no Z:
  - .NET's JSON deserialization gives 2026-08-01T00:00:00+03:00 on this machine.
    In August this machine is on Israel summer time, UTC+3, so the string is
    read as local midnight there.
  - In UTC: that instant is 2026-07-31T21:00:00Z, three hours earlier than the
    supplier meant.
  - DateTimeOffset.Parse with defaults behaves the same way.
  - With DateTimeStyles.AssumeUniversal, the same string gives +00:00, which is
    what we want.

  Why .NET does this: a DateTimeOffset must have an offset, and a string without
  one doesn't carry any. Rather than reject it, .NET fills the gap with the
  offset of whatever time zone the program runs in. With Z or +02:00 there's
  nothing to guess, which is why those two rows were fine.

  Why it matters for us:
  - Same input, different data: the same payload would be stored as a different
    instant on a server in another time zone, and on a UTC server it would look
    correct.
  - Our dedup rule: one supplier sending the same reservation with and without Z
    would look like a change, or two instances in different time zones would
    disagree.
  - updatedAtUtc: the version comparison and the far-future check depend on it
    being the real instant, so a shifted value would wrongly accept or reject
    updates.

  So I'd parse with AssumeUniversal, treating offset-less timestamps as UTC.
  They behave identically on every machine, and a payload with Z and one without
  it produce the same stored value.

  Defaults confirmed: offset-less timestamps are treated as UTC, and supplierId
  is compared exactly. I'll start chunk 3 test-first.

● Chunk 3 is implemented and all 51 tests pass. Nothing is committed yet,
  awaiting your acceptance.

  What I built, in src/SupplierFeed.Api/Domain/:
  - IngestParser.Parse(body) returns one of three results.
    - Unattributable: malformed JSON, a non-object, or a missing, blank or
      non-string supplierId.
    - Invalid: it carries the supplierId plus every error found in one pass.
    - Valid: it carries a ValidatedIngest with UTC millisecond timestamps and an
      exact decimal price.
  - Rules:
    - Property names are case-insensitive.
    - Timestamps must be ISO 8601, and offset-less ones are UTC.
    - Prices must be a JSON number, zero or more.
    - checkOut must be after checkIn; that check is skipped when a date is
      unparsable, to avoid a duplicate error.
  - Responses:
    - IngestStatus, IngestDetail, IngestResponse and ErrorResponse.
    - IngestJson.Options serializes them with lowercase enum words, so you get
      "outofdate".

  Test run: the first run had 1 failure out of 51. .NET's default JSON encoder
  escaped > as \u003E, which is valid but ugly to read in raw responses. I
  switched to the relaxed encoder, because this API returns JSON only, so price 
  must be >= 0 stays readable.

  Test check: I broke the code on purpose three times and each break was caught,
  then I restored the code:
  - Dropping AssumeUniversal failed the offset-less timestamp test.
  - Allowing negative prices failed two tests.
  - Changing <= to < in the date check failed the equal-dates case.

  One caveat on the offset-less timestamp test: it only catches the bug on a
  machine that isn't on UTC. On a UTC CI runner it would pass either way. It
  fails here because this machine is on Israel time.

  Chunk 3 is marked done in plan.md. Do you accept? If so I'll commit and push,
  then describe chunk 4, the throttle store, before starting it.

  What changed:
  - Field names: the seven JSON property names (supplierId, reservationId, and
    so on) were scattered as string literals. They're now constants in a private
    Field class.

    built from the Field constants, for example checkOut must be after checkIn.
    The per-field ones, such as is required and must be a number, are small
    helper methods.

    TimestampExample constant.

  I left the two timestamp format strings as a named TimestampFormats field.
  They're the contract definition itself, so they have nowhere better to live.
  The tests still use plain string literals for field names. That is
  intentional: they check the real wire names independently, so a typo in a
  constant would fail them.

  aren't in the parser. They'll be configuration in chunk 4, the throttle store.

● Chunk 3 is committed and pushed as ba0a8eb. Next, chunk 4 described before I
  implement it.

  Chunk 4: throttle store

  Goal: the component that decides, per supplier, whether a request is admitted
  or rejected. It is the core of the rate limiter. It has no HTTP or reservation
  logic.

  API: ThrottleStore.TryAdmit(supplierId) returns either Admitted or
  Throttled(retryAfterSeconds). It is synchronous. Microsoft.Data.Sqlite isn't
  truly async, so a fake async would only add overhead.

  What it does, in one transaction (the default BeginTransaction() is immediate,
  per the spike):
  1. Run the atomic insert from the plan: add a request_log row stamped with DB
     time, only if fewer than the limit rows exist in the window.
  2. If it inserted a row: commit and return Admitted.
  3. If it inserted nothing:
     - Increment that supplier's throttled counter, creating the stats row if
       absent.
     - Compute the retry time from the oldest in-window row, as oldest + window 
       - now, rounded up to whole seconds with a minimum of 1.
     - Commit and return Throttled.
     - Throttled requests add no log row, so hammering never extends the block.

  Settings: ThrottleOptions holds Limit (default 100) and WindowSeconds (default
  60), bound from the Throttle config section and validated as positive.
  They're read from configuration instead of being hard-coded, in line with your
  last change.

  Files: Data/ThrottleStore.cs, Data/ThrottleOptions.cs, and registration in
  Program.cs.

  Tests, written first: against a temp DB. Time is controlled by inserting
  backdated rows using the DB clock (now - N ms), with safe margins of 30s and
  61s, never near the 60s boundary, so nothing is flaky.
  - The first 100 are admitted and the 101st is throttled.
  - A throttled request adds no log row, and each one increments throttled by
    exactly 1, creating the stats row on first use.
  - 100 rows older than the window mean a new request is admitted.
  - With 99 recent rows and 1 expired, one request is admitted and the next is
    throttled.
  - retryAfter is about 30 when the oldest row is 30s old, within a 1-second
    tolerance.
  - A supplier at its limit doesn't affect another supplier or that supplier's
    counters.
  - A small custom limit (3) is honored.
  - 150 parallel admits on separate connections give exactly 100 admitted and no
    exceptions. This is a quick version of the spike. The full concurrency
    suite stays in chunk 8.

  Not in this chunk: the cleanup job (chunk 9), validation or reservation logic,
  and the endpoint.

  Decisions I'm making by default: a synchronous API, and Retry-After as a whole
  number of seconds rounded up with a minimum of 1. Should I go ahead with
  chunk 4 as described?

✻ Cogitated for 31s · done Wednesday 9:48 PM

❯ but the throttle happens only one valid ingest, we said invalid goes to stats 
  and also counts in rate limits, but it shouldnt be written to db of course    

● I think two things are getting mixed together, so here is where the plan
  stands before I change anything.

  What the plan says today:
  - Throttle order: the throttle runs before validation, on every attributable
    request. Chunk 4 only needs a supplierId, never the payload, so it can't
    tell valid from invalid.
  - Invalid and the limit: an invalid request counts toward the 100 per 60
    seconds. It leaves a row in request_log, the rate-limit record.
  - Invalid and reservations: an invalid request is never written to
    reservations.
  - Invalid and stats: the plan has invalid requests in no counter. Stats are
    {ingested, ignored, throttled}, and the README says invalid requests are "in
    none of them". You may be remembering a different decision, or you want it
    changed.

  Two points need your call.

  The request_log row. An invalid request has to leave a row there to count
  toward the limit across instances. A counter kept in memory per instance would
  break the exact-limit rule. No row would also mean no count, which
  contradicts "invalid counts in rate limits." That row is a record of the
  attempt, separate from the reservations table.

  An invalid counter. Adding one has a real upside. The four counters, ingested,
  ignored, invalid and throttled, would then add up to every attributable
  request. That fixes the oddity where the counters don't sum to total traffic.

● User answered Claude's questions:
  ⎿  · Should invalid (400) requests get their own counter in stats? → Yes, add 
     an `invalid` counter (Recommended)
     · Is it OK that an invalid request leaves a row in request_log (the 
     rate-limit record) but nothing in reservations? → Yes, request_log only 
     (Recommended)

  to the schema, update the response model so a 400 returns status: "invalid",

      16  ## Processing order for every ingest request
      17 -1. Parse body. No usable `supplierId` (malformed JSON, missing 
         -field) -> 400, nothing counted.                                
      18 -2. Throttle: atomic admit/reject (see Concurrency).            
      19 -3. Validate payload.                                           
      20 -4. Reject far-future `updatedAtUtc`.                           
      21 -5. Upsert reservation and classify the outcome.

         +er.Parse` reads the `supplierId` and validates the payload in o
         +ne pass. Result: Unattributable (no usable `supplierId`: 400, n
         +othing counted, no DB access), Invalid(supplierId), or Valid.  
      18 +2. **Throttle (DB):** atomic admit/reject for Invalid and Valid
         + requests. Rejected -> 429, `throttled`+1, even if the payload 
         +was invalid (it was never admitted).

         +e same transaction as the admit (the verdict is already known),
         + so no second transaction.

         +gainst DB time (400, `invalid`+1 if it fails), then upsert the 
         +reservation and classify the outcome.

● The invalid counter and the corrected flow are in, and all 51 tests pass.
  Nothing is committed yet.

  Code (amends the committed chunks 2 and 3):
  - Schema: supplier_stats has a new invalid counter column. There are no
    deployed databases yet, so no migration is needed.
  - Response model: IngestStatus gains Invalid, and the 400 body is now
    {"status":"invalid","errors":[...]}. The response status matches the
    counter, as you asked earlier.
  - Tests: I updated the two affected ones and watched them fail before the fix.

  Docs: plan.md, the README and the terminology lines in CLAUDE.md all describe
  the corrected flow.
  1. Parse and validate: a pure, in-memory step that reads the supplierId and
     validates in one pass. The result is Unattributable, Invalid or Valid.
  2. Throttle: it runs on every attributable request. A throttled request gets
     429 and throttled+1, even when its payload was invalid.
  3. If admitted and Invalid: 400 and invalid+1, written in the same transaction
     as the admit, since the verdict is already known.
  4. If admitted and Valid: the far-future check, then the reservation upsert. A
     far-future timestamp also counts as invalid, but that's only known inside
     the apply step because it needs the DB clock.

  The four counters now partition every attributable request, so they add up to
  a supplier's total traffic.

  Effect on chunk 4: because the invalid verdict is known before the throttle,
  TryAdmit takes a flag saying the request is already known to be invalid. When
  it admits such a request, it bumps invalid in the same transaction. I'd add
  tests for that: an invalid request counts toward the limit, increments invalid
  and not ingested, and writes nothing to reservations. Throttled invalid
  requests increment only throttled.

  Do you want me to commit these amendments now, as a small commit separate from
  chunk 4? Then I'll start chunk 4 with the flag included.

✻ Sautéed for 1m 36s · done Wednesday 10:32 PM

❯ the throttle logic should get only valid or invalid messages. the way i see   
  it is that it returns whether the message was throttled or not, the           
  orchestrator uses the validation logic, the throttle logic, and based on the  
  type of the request (valid, invalid) it writes it to reservations, then it    
  also updates the stats. tell me what you think                                

● I agree with the shape. I'd add one refinement and flag one decision it
  reverses.

  What I agree with: the throttle component should know nothing about validity,
  stats or reservations. Its whole contract is TryAdmit(supplierId), which
  returns Admitted or Throttled(retryAfterSeconds). That makes it simpler and
  easier to test than my version, where I passed it an "already invalid" flag.
  The orchestrator sequences everything:
  1. Parse and validate. If the result is Unattributable, return 400 and stop.
     The throttle never sees it.
  2. Throttle. If throttled, count throttled and return 429.
  3. If admitted and Invalid, count invalid and return 400.
  4. If admitted and Valid, write the reservation, then count ingested or
     ignored and return the response.

  Refinement: the orchestrator owns one transaction per request, and the stores 
  take the connection and transaction as arguments.
  - If each store opens its own transaction, a request takes the writer lock two
    or three times. That's the redundant-transaction cost you pointed out in
    the cleanup discussion. It also leaves gaps: a crash between the reservation
    write and the counter update leaves stats permanently wrong.
  - With one transaction, the slot row, the reservation write and the counter
    commit or roll back together. The stores stay simple and independent, since
    they just run SQL on the connection they're given. The orchestrator is the
    only place that knows the order.
  - A throttled request still needs only one transaction: the admit attempt, the
    throttled counter and the retry-after lookup.

  What this reverses:
  - Earlier decision: I'd chosen separate transactions for the throttle admit
    and the reservation write, so a crash between them would burn a quota slot.
  - New behavior: a failure after admission, such as a 500, rolls back the whole
    transaction, including the slot row. A failing request doesn't consume
    quota, and stats can't drift.
  - Trade-off: the writer lock is held a few milliseconds longer per request.
    That's offset by one lock acquisition instead of two, and nothing slow runs
    inside it.

  What it changes in the plan:
  - Chunk 4: shrinks to just ThrottleStore. The counter increments move out of
    it.
  - New StatsStore: it has an increment method called by the orchestrator, plus
    the read side for the stats endpoint. It would arrive with the orchestrator
    chunk.
  - Far-future check: it needs DB time, so a tiny DB clock helper would serve
    the orchestrator.
  - plan.md: the "Server error: slot burnt" row and the "separate transactions"
    note both change.

  My recommendation is to adopt this design. Say go and I'll update plan.md and
  the chunk list, commit the pending invalid amendments as their own small
  commit, then start chunk 4 as the pure throttle store. Or tell me which part
  you'd change first.
✻ Cooked for 29s · done Wednesday 10:39 PM

❯ why you change your mind on one transaction per request?                      

● My earlier choice of two transactions wasn't carefully reasoned, and I'm
  changing my mind because your design made the cost clear.

  The case for separate transactions was weak. I picked them by default, mainly
  to keep the writer lock held for less time, and I listed "a crash between them
  burns a quota slot" as an accepted cost. I never measured the lock-hold
  difference, and my "a few milliseconds" estimate is untested. I also missed a
  second cost: a crash between the two transactions leaves an admitted request
  with no counter increment, so stats drift permanently.

  Three things changed my mind:
  1. Stats counters are now part of the design. An invalid counter and
     per-outcome counters mean more state that has to stay consistent with what
     was actually done. One transaction makes that consistency automatic.
  2. Your orchestrator design. If the orchestrator coordinates independent
     stores, they need to share a connection anyway. Giving each its own
     transaction would add boundaries for no benefit.
  3. Your earlier objection to redundant transactions. The valid path would take
     the writer lock twice per request, and SQLite has one writer.

  A downside I glossed over in my last message: I presented "a failed request
  frees its quota slot" as a benefit. It's also a hole. If something fails
  persistently, such as a bug that makes every apply throw, a supplier retrying
  could send unlimited requests that each roll back and never count toward the
  limit. The throttle exists to stop exactly that kind of retry storm.

  The fix keeps one transaction and the old "slot is burnt" behavior:
  - Take the admit slot in the transaction, then run the apply step inside a
    SQLite SAVEPOINT.
  - If the apply step throws, roll back to the savepoint, which undoes the
    reservation write and counter changes. Then commit the transaction with the
    slot row still in it, and return 500.
  - The slot is consumed and no half-applied state remains.

  That's slightly more code than a plain transaction, but it gives atomic stats
  and a failure path that can't be abused. I'd add a test for it, where a forced
  apply failure leaves one extra request_log row and no reservation or counter
  changes.

  Should I go with one transaction plus the savepoint? If you'd rather keep it
  simple and accept the hole, I can use a plain transaction and document it as a
  known limitation.

※ recap: We're building the supplier throttle and dedup service chunk by chunk, with chunks 1 to 3 pushed and the invalid-counter amendments uncommitted. Right now I'm waiting on your call: one transaction per request with a savepoint, or a plain one.

❯ not yet, show me the tradeoffs between the 2 implementations                  

● Here are the two designs side by side. A is separate transactions, as in the
  original plan. B is one transaction per request owned by the orchestrator,
  with a savepoint around the apply step.

  Writer-lock acquisitions (valid request)
  A: separate transactions: 2: admit, then apply
  B: one transaction, savepoint around apply: 1
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Throttled request
  A: separate transactions: 1
  B: one transaction, savepoint around apply: 1
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Invalid request
  A: separate transactions: 1 for admit, plus another to write the invalid
  counter unless I fold it in
  B: one transaction, savepoint around apply: 1
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Time the lock is held
  A: separate transactions: Shorter each time, but the second wait queues behind

  other writers again, so latency can spike under contention
  B: one transaction, savepoint around apply: One slightly longer hold, with no
  re-queue. The size of the difference is unmeasured
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Crash between admit and apply
  A: separate transactions: Slot is consumed and no counter is written, so stats

  drift permanently
  B: one transaction, savepoint around apply: Atomic: the slot, outcome and
  counter commit together or not at all
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Failure after admit (500)
  A: separate transactions: Slot stays consumed naturally
  B: one transaction, savepoint around apply: Same, but only because of the
  savepoint. A plain transaction would roll the slot back, and a persistent
  failure would let a supplier retry unthrottled
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Stats consistency
  A: separate transactions: Needs care at every boundary
  B: one transaction, savepoint around apply: Automatic
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  SQLITE_BUSY exposure
  A: separate transactions: Two chances to time out per request
  B: one transaction, savepoint around apply: One
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Stores
  A: separate transactions: Each opens its own connection and transaction.
  Simple
  and independent
  B: one transaction, savepoint around apply: Each takes a connection and
  transaction as arguments. A bit more plumbing
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Orchestrator
  A: separate transactions: Just sequences calls
  B: one transaction, savepoint around apply: Owns the transaction and
  savepoint,
  so it's more code and needs a failure-injection test
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Testing
  A: separate transactions: Stores test in complete isolation
  B: one transaction, savepoint around apply: Stores test via a small
  connection-and-transaction helper, plus one extra test for the savepoint path
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  SQL Server portability
  A: separate transactions: Straightforward
  B: one transaction, savepoint around apply: Savepoint syntax differs (SAVE 
  TRANSACTION), a small adapter later
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  If the window moves to Redis
  A: separate transactions: The throttle is already independent of the DB
  transaction, a natural fit
  B: one transaction, savepoint around apply: The throttle's SQL would sit
  inside
  the DB transaction. Redis could never join it, so we'd split it back out and
  wrap the throttle in an interface

  Where each wins:
  - A is simpler and more modular, and it matches the Redis future. Its weakness
    is that stats can silently drift after a crash, and the valid path pays two
    lock acquisitions.
  - B gives exact, consistent stats and one lock per request. Its weaknesses are
    more plumbing and a savepoint to get right, and the throttle becomes
    coupled to the DB transaction.

  What isn't known: I haven't measured the lock-hold cost for either. The spike
  showed 150 admits across 8 threads with no errors, but nothing on lock-hold
  time for a full request. If that matters to your decision, a small benchmark
  of both would answer it, but I'd skip it for this scope.

  My lean: B with the savepoint. The assignment cares about correctness under
  concurrency, and drifting stats are a real correctness bug. A is a defensible
  choice if you prefer simplicity and are happy to document the stats drift.
