      54  - Atomic check-and-insert in one statement: `INSERT ... SELECT 
          ... WHERE (count in window) < 100`. 0 rows affected -> throttle
          d. The DB stamps the time in the same statement; the app never 
          supplies "now".
      55  - Throttled -> increment the supplier's throttled counter in th
          e same transaction (`INSERT ... ON CONFLICT DO UPDATE`).
      56  - Reservation upsert: unique index on `(supplierId, reservation
          Id)`; conditional `UPDATE ... WHERE updatedAtUtc <= @new AND de
          tails differ`, so concurrent writers can't regress data.
      57 -- Throttle admit and reservation upsert are separate transactio
         -ns (a crash between burns a quota slot; accepted).             
      57 +- **One transaction per request, owned by the orchestrator** (c
         +hosen over separate transactions per step): the admit row, the 
         +reservation write and the stats counter commit or roll back tog
         +ether, so stats can't drift after a crash, and the writer lock 
         +is taken once. The apply step runs inside a `SAVEPOINT`: if it 
         +throws, roll back to the savepoint and commit the admit row, so
         + a failing request still consumes its quota slot (otherwise a p
         +ersistent failure would let a supplier retry unthrottled) and r
         +eturns 500. Stores take the connection and transaction as argum
         +ents and never open their own.                                 
      58 +- Trade-offs accepted: throttle SQL is coupled to the DB transa
         +ction (a Redis window store could not join it, so it would be s
         +plit out behind an interface then); SQL Server needs `SAVE TRAN
         +SACTION` instead of `SAVEPOINT`; lock-hold time per request is 
         +slightly longer (unmeasured).                                  
      59  - `Retry-After` comes from the oldest row in the window.
      60  - Avoid `SQLITE_BUSY` between instances with immediate transact
          ions, WAL, and a finite `Default Timeout` (see "SQLite facts" b
          elow).
      61  - Cleanup: a `BackgroundService` per instance, roughly once a m
          inute, runs one global `DELETE FROM request_log WHERE tsMs <= n
          ow - 60000` (DB clock). Not per request: a per-request delete a
          dds lock time to SQLite's single writer and is redundant across
           instances. Correctness never depends on it (the count query on
          ly reads in-window rows via the `(supplierId, tsMs)` index, so 
          old rows don't slow it). Idempotent, so overlapping instances a
          re harmless. No TTL option exists on SQLite (no row expiry, no 
          event scheduler; a trigger would just be per-insert cost inside
           the DB), so the in-service job is a compromise; the preferred 
          design is a single external cleaner (cron job / DB-side schedul
          er) — see README. Also cleans rows from quiet/spoofed suppliers
          . Table stays ~100-200 rows per supplier.

● The README tracks key decisions and disagreements, so I'm adding this one
  there too, briefly.

● Update(README.md)
  ⎿  Added 1 line
       9  - **The database is the single source of truth** for the window
           state and the clock (one atomic check-and-insert). On SQLite t
          his only holds on one host: SQLite's "now" is the app process's
           OS clock.
      10  - **Identity** is `(supplierId, reservationId)`; `updatedAtUtc`
           is the version. An older version with different details is ign
          ored as `outofdate`.
      11  - **Throttle runs before validation**; every attributable reque
          st counts, throttled ones don't occupy the window.
      12 +- **One transaction per request**, owned by the orchestrator: t
         +he throttle slot, reservation write and stats counter commit to
         +gether, so stats can't drift after a crash. The apply step runs
         + in a savepoint so a failing request still consumes its quota s
         +lot.                                                           
      13  - **Responses and stats share one vocabulary:** `ingested` (cre
          ated/updated), `ignored` (duplicate/outofdate), `invalid` (400)
          , `throttled` (429 + `Retry-After`). The counters add up to a s
          upplier's total traffic; invalid requests count toward the limi
          t but never touch `reservations`.

      15  ## Where I disagreed with / changed Claude Code's suggestions

● Update(README.md)
  ⎿  Added 1 line
      14  
      15  ## Where I disagreed with / changed Claude Code's suggestions
      16  - Claude proposed per-instance clocks with accepted skew. Rejec
          ted: it breaks the single-source-of-truth and exact-limit assum
          ptions. Switched to DB-stamped time.
      17 +- Claude planned separate transactions for the throttle admit a
         +nd the reservation write, as a default it hadn't stress-tested.
         + I steered to one transaction per request after weighing the tr
         +ade-offs: it gives consistent stats and one lock acquisition, a
         +t the cost of coupling the throttle to the DB transaction (a Re
         +dis window store would have to be split back out). Claude's fir
         +st single-transaction version would have let failing requests s
         +kip the limit; the savepoint closes that.                      
      18  - Claude proposed deleting old window rows on every request, th
          en a cleanup job inside each service instance. I disagreed with
           both: per-request deletes add write load on every call, and N 
          instances each running their own cleaner is redundant. SQLite h
          as no row TTL or scheduler, so the in-service job stays only be
          cause the fixed stack leaves no alternative.
      19  
      20  ## What I'd do differently with more time

  │ 7   │ Stats endpoint      │ Both HTTP endpoints                         │
  └─────┴─────────────────────┴─────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Chunks 8 to 10 are unchanged.

  Chunk 4: throttle store (pure)

  Goal: decide admit or reject for a supplier, and nothing else. It knows
  nothing about validity, stats or reservations. That's the decision you made.

  API: ThrottleStore.TryAdmit(connection, transaction, supplierId) returns
  Admitted or Throttled(retryAfterSeconds). It runs on the connection and
  transaction it's given and never opens its own, which is what makes the single
  transaction in B possible.

  What it does:
  - Admit: the atomic insert from the plan. It adds a request_log row stamped
    with DB time, only if the supplier has fewer than the limit rows in the
    window.
  - Reject: it adds no row and computes retryAfterSeconds from the oldest
    in-window row, as oldest + window - now, rounded up with a minimum of 1.
  - Stats: it doesn't touch stats. The orchestrator increments throttled when it
    sees a rejection, in chunk 6.

  Settings: ThrottleOptions holds Limit (default 100) and WindowSeconds (default
  60). They're bound from the Throttle config section, validated as positive,
  and registered in Program.cs. The store doesn't hard-code them.

  Files: Data/ThrottleStore.cs, Data/ThrottleOptions.cs and the Program.cs
  registration, plus a small test helper that opens a connection and
  transaction.

  Tests, written first against a temp DB, with time controlled by backdated rows
  using the DB clock and safe margins of 30s and 61s:
  - The first 100 are admitted and the 101st is rejected.
  - A rejection adds no row.
  - 100 rows older than the window mean a new request is admitted.
  - With 99 recent rows and 1 expired, one request is admitted and the next is
    rejected.
  - retryAfterSeconds is about 30 when the oldest row is 30s old, within a
    1-second tolerance.
  - A supplier at its limit doesn't affect another supplier.
  - A custom limit of 3 is honored.
  - Rolling back the transaction removes the admit row. This is a first test of
    the behavior B relies on.
  - 150 parallel admits on separate connections, each in its own transaction,
    give exactly 100 admitted and no exceptions. This is a quick version of the
    spike. The full multi-instance suite stays in chunk 8.

  Not in this chunk: stats counters, reservations, validation, the orchestrator
  and the HTTP endpoints.

  Do you accept this definition? When you say go, should I also commit the
  pending invalid-counter amendments as their own small commit first? I'd do
  that so chunk 4 starts from a clean tree.

✻ Worked for 47s · done Wednesday 10:56 PM

  What I built, in src/SupplierFeed.Api/Data/:
  - ThrottleStore.TryAdmit(connection, transaction, supplierId) returns Admitted
    or Throttled(retryAfterSeconds).
    - The count and insert are one SQL statement with DB-stamped time, as
      designed.
    - It runs on the caller's connection and transaction. It never opens its
      own, and it knows nothing about stats, validity or reservations.
    - On a rejection it adds no row and works out the retry time from the oldest
      in-window row, rounded up to whole seconds.
  - ThrottleOptions holds the limit and window, defaulting to 100 and 60.
    They're bound from the Throttle config section. Non-positive values fail at
    startup, because Program.cs builds the store eagerly.
  - ThrottleDecision is a small result type, in the same style as the parser
    result.

  Tests: 15 new ones, 66 in total.
  - Limit behavior: 100 admitted and the 101st rejected, a rejection adds no
    row, expired rows don't count, and exactly one slot opens when one row
    expires.
  - Retry-after: it is exactly 30 for rows 30s old.
  - Isolation and settings: suppliers are independent, custom limit and window
    are honored, invalid options are rejected, and the config binding works end
    to end through the app.
  - Rollback: a rolled-back transaction removes the admit row.
  - Concurrency: 150 parallel admits give exactly 100 admitted, and I ran that
    test six more times with no flakes.

  Test check: I broke the code on purpose three times. Using <= and ignoring the
  window were both caught, and a restored version passes. The third mutation,
  rounding the retry time down, was not caught. The test I'd written accepted 29
  to 31 seconds, so I tightened it to exactly 30 and confirmed the mutation now
  fails. I also removed a redundant Math.Max(1, …) from the code, since a
  positive remainder rounded up is already at least 1.

  Gap: the rare case where the oldest row expires between the failed admit and
  the retry-time lookup, which returns 1, can't be tested deterministically.
  It's one line, but it isn't covered by a test.

  plan.md marks chunk 4 as done, awaiting acceptance. Do you accept? If so I'll
  commit and push, then describe chunk 5, the reservation store with the DB
  clock helper.

✻ Cogitated for 3m 47s · done Wednesday 11:01 PM

❯ don't round the retry time, we want to give the user an accurate retry time   

● That's a good call. One constraint to flag first: the HTTP Retry-After header
  is defined as a whole number of seconds, so it can't carry fractions. The
  response body can, so I'm making the store return the exact remaining time in
  milliseconds. The body will carry that exact value, and the header will round
  up only because the protocol forces it. Tests first.

● Update(plan.md)
  ⎿  Added 1 line, removed 1 line
      56  - Reservation upsert: unique index on `(supplierId, reservation
          Id)`; conditional `UPDATE ... WHERE updatedAtUtc <= @new AND de
          tails differ`, so concurrent writers can't regress data.
      57  - **One transaction per request, owned by the orchestrator** (c
          hosen over separate transactions per step): the admit row, the 
          reservation write and the stats counter commit or roll back tog
          ether, so stats can't drift after a crash, and the writer lock 
          is taken once. The apply step runs inside a `SAVEPOINT`: if it 
          throws, roll back to the savepoint and commit the admit row, so
           a failing request still consumes its quota slot (otherwise a p
          ersistent failure would let a supplier retry unthrottled) and r
          eturns 500. Stores take the connection and transaction as argum
          ents and never open their own.
      58  - Trade-offs accepted: throttle SQL is coupled to the DB transa
          ction (a Redis window store could not join it, so it would be s
          plit out behind an interface then); SQL Server needs `SAVE TRAN
          SACTION` instead of `SAVEPOINT`; lock-hold time per request is 
          slightly longer (unmeasured).
      59 -- `Retry-After` comes from the oldest row in the window.       
      59 +- Retry time comes from the oldest row in the window and is exa
         +ct (milliseconds), never rounded by the store. The 429 body als
         +o states the configured rule (`limit`, `windowSeconds`) so the 
         +supplier knows why, even after the configuration changes. Only 
         +the HTTP `Retry-After` header is rounded up to whole seconds, b
         +ecause the protocol cannot carry fractions.                    
      60  - Avoid `SQLITE_BUSY` between instances with immediate transact
          ions, WAL, and a finite `Default Timeout` (see "SQLite facts" b
          elow).
      61  - Cleanup: a `BackgroundService` per instance, roughly once a m
          inute, runs one global `DELETE FROM request_log WHERE tsMs <= n
          ow - 60000` (DB clock). Not per request: a per-request delete a
          dds lock time to SQLite's single writer and is redundant across
           instances. Correctness never depends on it (the count query on
          ly reads in-window rows via the `(supplierId, tsMs)` index, so 
          old rows don't slow it). Idempotent, so overlapping instances a
          re harmless. No TTL option exists on SQLite (no row expiry, no 
          event scheduler; a trigger would just be per-insert cost inside
           the DB), so the in-service job is a compromise; the preferred 
          design is a single external cleaner (cron job / DB-side schedul
          er) — see README. Also cleans rows from quiet/spoofed suppliers
          . Table stays ~100-200 rows per supplier.
      62

● Both changes are in, and all 67 tests pass. Chunk 4 is still uncommitted,
  awaiting your acceptance.

  Exact retry time
  - Store: ThrottleDecision.Throttled now carries RetryAfterMs, the exact
    milliseconds until the oldest in-window request expires, with no rounding.
    If that row already expired between the failed admit and the lookup, it
    returns 0.
  - Response body: the 429 body carries the same exact value as retryAfterMs.
  - Retry-After header: this is the one place a rounded value is unavoidable.
    HTTP defines the header as a whole number of seconds, so chunk 7 will set it
    to the exact time rounded up, which means a client that honors it never
    retries early.
  - Test check: the retry-after test now requires a value strictly below
    30,000ms, after a short sleep so the elapsed time is guaranteed. Rounding
    the value up to whole seconds fails it.

  The reason for the limit
  - Throttled now also carries the configured Limit and WindowSeconds, and the
    429 body includes them as limit and windowSeconds. A throttled response
    looks like
    {"status":"throttled","retryAfterMs":29940,"limit":100,"windowSeconds":60}.
  - The values come from the store's configuration. If the configuration
    changes, the response changes with it.
  - Test check: replacing them with hard-coded 100 and 60 fails the new test,
    which uses a limit of 3 over 20 seconds.
  - Human-readable sentence: I added structured fields, not a prose message. If
    you also want a sentence like "Rate limit exceeded: 100 requests per 60
    seconds", I can build it from those fields when the HTTP layer is written in
    chunk 7.

  plan.md was updated in three places: the throttled row, the retry bullet and
  the chunk 4 and 7 descriptions. Do you accept chunk 4? If so I'll commit and
  push, then describe chunk 5.

※ recap: We're building the supplier feed throttle and dedup service in chunks, and chunk 4 (the throttle store, with exact retry time and the limit reason in the 429) is done, 67 tests passing, uncommitted. Next: you accept it, then I commit, push, and describe chunk 5.

❯ this could end up in a bad ux, a user can be throttled and always get that    
  retry after is 0                                                              
● You're right to worry, and checking it showed a real flaw in my approach.

  When a throttled supplier can get 0:
  - How it happens: the store makes two separate reads of the clock, one for the
    admit and a slightly later one for the retry lookup. If the oldest request
    leaves the window between those two reads, the second read finds the window
    already moving.
  - The second flaw is worse: the lookup then reports the expiry of the
    next-oldest request. That overstates the wait when a slot has actually just
    freed.
  - Frequency: it isn't "always". The race window is a few milliseconds, but
    it's a bad contract. I wrote "0 if it already has" in the docs, and a client
    told 0 retries immediately.
  - The cause: the decision and the retry time were computed at different
    instants.

  The fix: read the DB clock once and use that single instant for the count, the
  insert and the retry time.
  - Retry time guaranteed positive: every row counted at that instant satisfies
    ts > now - window, so ts + window - now is always above 0. The retry time is
    exact and consistent with the decision, with no floor and no rounding.
  - Cost: one cheap in-memory clock read per request. It still comes from the
    DB, so the "app never invents the time" rule holds.
  - Deterministic tests: an internal TryAdmitAt(…, nowMs) lets tests fix the
    time and place rows at exact timestamps. They can then check boundaries I
    could only approximate before: a row 1ms from expiry gives exactly 1, and a
    row exactly at the window edge is outside the window.

  I'm writing the tests first.

● Update(plan.md)
  ⎿  Added 1 line
      56  - Reservation upsert: unique index on `(supplierId, reservation
          Id)`; conditional `UPDATE ... WHERE updatedAtUtc <= @new AND de
          tails differ`, so concurrent writers can't regress data.
      57  - **One transaction per request, owned by the orchestrator** (c
          hosen over separate transactions per step): the admit row, the 
          reservation write and the stats counter commit or roll back tog
          ether, so stats can't drift after a crash, and the writer lock 
          is taken once. The apply step runs inside a `SAVEPOINT`: if it 
          throws, roll back to the savepoint and commit the admit row, so
           a failing request still consumes its quota slot (otherwise a p
          ersistent failure would let a supplier retry unthrottled) and r
          eturns 500. Stores take the connection and transaction as argum
          ents and never open their own.
      58  - Trade-offs accepted: throttle SQL is coupled to the DB transa
          ction (a Redis window store could not join it, so it would be s
          plit out behind an interface then); SQL Server needs `SAVE TRAN
          SACTION` instead of `SAVEPOINT`; lock-hold time per request is 
          slightly longer (unmeasured).
      59 +- The throttle reads the DB clock once per decision and uses th
         +at single instant for the count, the insert and the retry time.
         + Measuring the retry time at a later instant could report 0 (or
         + the wrong, later row's expiry) when the oldest row expires bet
         +ween two reads; with one instant the retry time is always posit
         +ive and consistent with the decision. Tests fix the instant thr
         +ough an internal `TryAdmitAt`.                                 
      60  - Retry time comes from the oldest row in the window and is exa
          ct (milliseconds), never rounded by the store. The 429 body als
          o states the configured rule (`limit`, `windowSeconds`) so the 
          supplier knows why, even after the configuration changes. Only 
          the HTTP `Retry-After` header is rounded up to whole seconds, b
          ecause the protocol cannot carry fractions.
      61  - Avoid `SQLITE_BUSY` between instances with immediate transact
          ions, WAL, and a finite `Default Timeout` (see "SQLite facts" b
          elow).
      62  - Cleanup: a `BackgroundService` per instance, roughly once a m
          inute, runs one global `DELETE FROM request_log WHERE tsMs <= n
          ow - 60000` (DB clock). Not per request: a per-request delete a
          dds lock time to SQLite's single writer and is redundant across
           instances. Correctness never depends on it (the count query on
          ly reads in-window rows via the `(supplierId, tsMs)` index, so 
          old rows don't slow it). Idempotent, so overlapping instances a
          re harmless. No TTL option exists on SQLite (no row expiry, no 
          event scheduler; a trigger would just be per-insert cost inside
           the DB), so the in-service job is a compromise; the preferred 
          design is a single external cleaner (cron job / DB-side schedul
          er) — see README. Also cleans rows from quiet/spoofed suppliers
          . Table stays ~100-200 rows per supplier.

● The retry time in a throttled response is now always above zero, and all 72
  tests pass. Nothing from chunk 4 is committed yet.

  What I changed
  - One clock read per decision. The store reads the DB clock once and uses that
    instant for the count, the insert and the retry time. The retry time is
    oldest row's timestamp + window - that instant. Every row counted at that
    instant is inside the window, so the result is always at least 1 ms. A
    throttled supplier can never be told 0.
  - The old flaw is gone. It could report 0, or the wait for the next-oldest
    request, when the oldest request expired between two clock reads. The "0 if
    already expired" fallback and its null case are removed. If the window is
    somehow empty at that point, the store now throws, because that would mean a
    programming error such as a non-write transaction.
  - Cost: one cheap in-memory clock read per request. It still comes from the
    DB, so the app never invents the time.
  - Public API unchanged. TryAdmit works as before, and an internal
    TryAdmitAt(…, nowMs) is what tests use, via InternalsVisibleTo.

  New deterministic tests, with the clock fixed and rows at exact timestamps.
  This replaced my earlier sleep-and-range approximation.
  - Rows 30s old give exactly 30,000ms. A row 1ms from expiring gives exactly 1,
    never 0.
  - The retry time follows the oldest row, not the newest.
  - A row exactly one window old is outside the window.
  - Retrying after the reported time is admitted, and retrying 1ms early is
    still throttled.

  Test check: four deliberate breaks were all caught: an inclusive window edge,
  a retry time off by one, rounding up to whole seconds, and the old bug of
  measuring from a later instant.

  One thing worth knowing: Retry-After on the HTTP header will still round up to
  whole seconds, as before. That's the protocol limit and it can only be 1 or
  more now.

  plan.md records the single-instant rule. Do you accept chunk 4? If so I'll
  commit and push, then describe chunk 5.

✻ Worked for 2m 47s · done Wednesday 11:14 PM

❯ so its still 2 separate querys but now it uses the same db clock? my issue is 
  with the gap between those queries, couldn't it make the same bug?            

● Yes, it's still separate statements: a clock read, the admit, and on rejection
  the oldest-row lookup. You're right to look at the gap. It can't cause the
  same bug, but only because of a condition I hadn't made explicit.

  Why the gap is harmless here: the old bug needed something to move between the
  statements, and now two things are frozen.
  - Time is frozen. All statements use the same nowMs and never read the clock
    again. Rows can't expire relative to a fixed instant.
  - Data is frozen. The transaction holds the write lock. No other connection
    can add or remove rows until it commits, so the count and the oldest-row
    lookup see the same state.

  Together, the three statements all see "the log at instant N", so the throttle
  decision and the retry time agree.

  The condition I hadn't made explicit: the guarantee depends on the lock being
  taken before the clock is read.
  - When it holds: the spike showed the default BeginTransaction() is immediate.
    It takes the write lock at BEGIN, before any statement runs.
  - When it breaks: if a caller used a deferred transaction, the clock read
    would happen first. The request could then wait seconds for the lock, and
    nowMs would be stale when it finally wrote. The row would be stamped too
    early and could expire a little early, so a later request might be admitted
    slightly too soon. That would breach the exact-limit rule.

  The store can't inspect which kind of transaction it was given, so I'm adding
  a regression test for the default and documenting the precondition on the
  method. That way a library change or a careless caller is caught.

● The several statements can't reproduce the old bug, as long as the transaction
  already holds the write lock when the clock is read. I've now pinned that
  condition with a test, and all 73 tests pass. Nothing from chunk 4 is
  committed yet.

  Why the gap is harmless: the throttle runs a clock read, the admit, and on
  rejection the oldest-row lookup, so it's still separate statements. All of
  them use the same instant, and the write lock is held for the whole
  transaction, so no other writer can change the log in between. The earlier bug
  needed the time or the data to move between statements, and neither can now.

  What I added to cover the condition:
  - Regression test: one connection opens the default BeginTransaction() and
    runs no statement. A second connection with a 1-second timeout then fails to
    write with SQLITE_BUSY. That proves the lock is taken at BEGIN. Switching
    the holder to a deferred transaction makes the test fail, so it does guard
    the condition.
  - Precondition note: the doc comment on TryAdmit says the caller's transaction
    must already hold the write lock. It says why, and what goes wrong with a
    deferred transaction: a stale instant and an admit row stamped too early.
    plan.md records the same.

  What it can't do: the store can't inspect which kind of transaction it was

✻ Worked for 1m 10s · done Wednesday 11:19 PM

❯ my idea was one query that returns the retry, if its 0 you know its emmited.  
  consider the trade offs here                                                  

● Short answer: SQLite can't do it in one statement, but a one-query version of
  your idea is cheaper than what I have. I'd adopt it.

  Why not literally one statement: INSERT … RETURNING only returns rows that
  were actually inserted. On a rejection it returns nothing, so the statement
  can't also report the retry time. SQLite also doesn't allow an INSERT inside a
  WITH, so I can't combine the write and a read into one. The only
  single-statement alternative is a per-supplier counter row, such as a token
  bucket. That would break the spec: a token bucket allows about 200 requests in
  some 60-second windows, not 100.

  Your design, adapted: one read decides, then an insert if there's a slot.
  1. One query returns the DB instant, the count in the window, and the oldest
     row.
  2. If the count is below the limit, the retry is 0, so insert at that instant
     and admit.
  3. Otherwise retry = oldest + window - now, and it's always positive.

  The 0 sentinel is safe because of the guarantee we just built. A throttled
  request can never have retry 0, so 0 always means "a slot was free".

  Statements on the admit path
  Current: 2 (clock, insert)
  Your idea, as one read plus insert: 2 (query, insert)
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Statements on the rejection path
  Current: 3 (clock, failed insert, oldest lookup)
  Your idea, as one read plus insert: 1
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Single time instant
  Current: yes
  Your idea, as one read plus insert: yes
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Atomicity
  Current: The insert is one statement with the count inside it, so it's atomic
  on its own
  Your idea, as one read plus insert: Relies on the write lock held across the
  read and the insert
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  If the lock precondition is violated
  Current: The admit row's timestamp may be slightly stale
  Your idea, as one read plus insert: I expect a loud error, "database is
  locked", not silent over-admission. The spike's deferred runs failed that
  way, but I haven't tested this exact sequence
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Where the decision lives
  Current: Split between SQL and C#
  Your idea, as one read plus insert: One place in C#, from three plain numbers
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Existing tests
  Current: n/a
  Your idea, as one read plus insert: All still apply unchanged, since the
  contract is identical

  The case for it: the rejection path is the hot one under abuse, and it drops
  from three statements to one. The admit path costs the same. It also uses the
  same clock read for everything and puts the decision in one readable spot.

  The case against it: exactness now depends on the write lock being held
  between the read and the insert. We already require that lock for time
  consistency and pin it with the regression test I just added, so this makes an
  existing dependency more important rather than adding a new one. The
  single-statement insert doesn't need the lock for atomicity.

  On SQL Server later: both versions need lock hints for the count to be atomic,
  so neither gains an advantage.

  My recommendation is to adopt it. I'd also add a test that a deferred
  transaction fails loudly rather than over-admitting, to confirm the "loud
  failure" claim instead of assuming it.

  Should I change the store to the one-read design?
✻ Cooked for 37s · done Wednesday 11:22 PM

❯ so that's 2 queries still                                                     

● Yes, on the admit path it's still two statements, same as the current code.
  The real gain was only on the rejection path. I should have been plainer about
  that.

  Can it be one statement for both outcomes? Not in SQLite without giving
  something up.
  - Marker row: write a row for every request, rejected ones included, flagged
    admitted or not, and return the flag with RETURNING. That brings back write
    amplification: a flooding supplier would write a row per rejected request.
    We avoided that on purpose. It would also need the count query and cleanup
    to ignore the flagged rows.
  - Counter row: a token bucket or similar breaks the "100 in any rolling 60s"
    rule, as explained earlier.

  So at least one path needs two statements. The question is which path gets
  one:

  ┌──────────────────┬───────────┬─────────┬───────────────────────────────┐
  │                  │  Admit    │ Reject  │             Notes             │
  │                  │   path    │  path   │                               │
  ├──────────────────┼───────────┼─────────┼───────────────────────────────┤
  │ Current          │ 2 (clock, │ 3       │ Simplest, tested              │
  │                  │  insert)  │         │                               │
  ├──────────────────┼───────────┼─────────┼───────────────────────────────┤
  │ Read, then       │           │         │ Cheaper under abuse. Depends  │
  │ insert (my last  │ 2         │ 1       │ on the lock between read and  │
  │ proposal)        │           │         │ insert                        │
  ├──────────────────┼───────────┼─────────┼───────────────────────────────┤
  │ Insert first,    │           │         │ Cheaper in normal traffic.    │
  │ read only on     │ 1         │ 2       │ Needs a small retry in the    │
  │ rejection        │           │         │ rare race where a slot frees  │
  │                  │           │         │ up                            │
  └──────────────────┴───────────┴─────────┴───────────────────────────────┘

  The third row is "try the atomic INSERT … RETURNING first, with the clock
  inside the statement. If it inserts, you're done in one statement. If it
  inserts nothing, run one read for the retry time, and if that read shows a
  slot is free after all, loop back and insert again." The loop runs at most
  twice, and throttled stays truthful because the second read confirms the
  window is full at its own instant.

  Statement count barely matters on SQLite. The database runs inside our
  process, so each statement costs microseconds, with no network round trip. The
  expensive part is the write itself and how long the lock is held, and all
  three variants do the same single insert. Statement count matters on a
  networked database such as SQL Server, where each statement is a round trip. I
  leaned on it more than it deserves in my last message.

  My honest recommendation: keep the current design. It's simple, fully tested,
  and exact. The extra statements are cheap here, and the other two variants
  trade clarity for micro-savings. If we move to SQL Server later, the "insert
  first" variant is the one to revisit, since it makes the common admit case a
  single round trip.

  Do you want to keep the current store, or switch to one of the other two? If
  you keep it, I'll commit and push chunk 4 and then describe chunk 5.

● Chunk 4 is committed and pushed as 5b47ec9, and the tree is clean. Chunk 5 is
  described below before I implement it.

  Chunk 5: reservation store and the future-timestamp rule

  Goal: decide what happens to a valid, already-admitted request: create it,
  update it, or leave it alone. The store runs on the caller's transaction, like
  the throttle store, and has no stats or HTTP code.

  ReservationStore.Apply(connection, transaction, ValidatedIngest) returns an
  IngestDetail, the enum we already have: Created, Updated, Duplicate or
  OutOfDate. Reusing it avoids a second enum that means the same thing. The
  logic is the request-types table from plan.md:

  ┌─────────┬────────────────────────────┬───────────┬─────────────────────┐
  │ Stored  │          Incoming          │  Result   │        Write        │
  │   row   │                            │           │                     │
  ├─────────┼────────────────────────────┼───────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │ none    │ any                        │ Created   │ insert              │
  ├─────────┼────────────────────────────┼───────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │ exists  │ same details, same or      │ Duplicate │ none                │
  │         │ older updatedAtUtc         │           │                     │
  ├─────────┼────────────────────────────┼───────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │ exists  │ same details, newer        │ Duplicate │ bump stored         │
  │         │ updatedAtUtc               │           │ updatedAtUtc only   │
  ├─────────┼────────────────────────────┼───────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │ exists  │ different details, older   │ OutOfDate │ none, stored data   │
  │         │ updatedAtUtc               │           │ kept                │
  ├─────────┼────────────────────────────┼───────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │ exists  │ different details, equal   │ Updated   │ replace all fields  │
  │         │ or newer updatedAtUtc      │           │                     │
  └─────────┴────────────────────────────┴───────────┴─────────────────────┘

  - Details means roomId, checkIn, checkOut and price.
  - Price is compared as a decimal, so 450.0 and 450.00 are the same.
  - Lookup: the stored row is found by (supplierId, reservationId).
  - Precondition: it relies on the same write-lock precondition as the throttle.
    The primary key is the backstop against a double insert.

  DB clock helper. A small DbClock.NowMs(connection, transaction) holds the
  clock query that ThrottleStore currently keeps as a private constant. I'd move
  that constant into the helper so there's one definition.

  Future-timestamp rule.
  - Policy: UpdatedAtPolicy is a small pure class. Given an updatedAtUtc and the
    DB's now, it returns an error message if the timestamp is too far ahead,
    and nothing otherwise.
  - Why it's separate from the store: it's validation, not storage. The
    orchestrator will read the DB clock, ask the policy, and report invalid if
    it objects.
  - Configurable: the allowed skew comes from a config section, default 300
    seconds, so it isn't hard-coded. The error message includes the configured
    value. Negative values fail at startup.

  Tests, written first
  - Reservation store: one test per row above. A "no write" check uses SQLite's
    total_changes(), so it proves nothing was written and not just that values
    look the same.
    - Created values round-trip, including the exact price.
    - Duplicates leave the row alone.
    - A newer-version duplicate changes only updatedAtUtc.
    - An older-version duplicate does not lower the stored timestamp.
    - Out-of-date keeps stored data.
    - Equal-version different details is Updated.
    - Each of the four detail fields changing on its own triggers Updated.
    - 450.0 against 450.00 is a duplicate.
    - The same reservationId under two suppliers makes two rows.
  - DB clock: it's close to the process clock and never goes backwards.
  - Future policy: exactly at the allowed skew passes, one millisecond over
    fails, the past is fine, and the message carries the configured value.
  - Config binding for the skew, checked through the app.

  Not in this chunk: stats counters, the orchestrator, the savepoint and the
  HTTP endpoints.

  Defaults I'm choosing: the future-skew limit is 300 seconds and configurable,
  and IngestDetail doubles as the store's result. Should I go ahead with chunk 5
  as described?

● Chunk 5 is implemented and all 88 tests pass. Nothing is committed yet,
  awaiting your acceptance.

  Plan change you asked for: I moved the future-timestamp check out of chunk 5.
  In plan.md it's now step 2 of the orchestrator flow, right after the pure
  validation and before the throttle. It completes the validity verdict before
  the throttle runs. The DB clock helper and UpdatedAtPolicy now belong to chunk
  6.

  What I built: ReservationStore.Apply(connection, transaction, ValidatedIngest)
  returns an IngestDetail.
  - No stored row: inserts it and returns Created.
  - Same details: returns Duplicate, and bumps the stored version only if the
    incoming one is newer. Otherwise it writes nothing.
  - Different details and an older version: returns OutOfDate and writes
    nothing.
  - Different details and an equal or newer version: replaces the row and
    returns Updated.
  - Lock precondition: it relies on the same write-lock precondition as the
    throttle store, and the primary key is the backstop against a double insert.
  - Registered: as a singleton in Program.cs.

  Tests: 15 new ones, 88 in total.
  - One test per row of the request-types table.
  - No-write checks use SQLite's total_changes(), so they prove nothing was
    written, not just that the values look unchanged.
  - Single-field changes: each of roomId, checkIn, checkOut and price changing
    on its own gives Updated.
  - Price comparison: 450.0 against 450.00 is a duplicate.
  - Isolation and rollback: the same reservationId under two suppliers makes two
    rows, and a rollback leaves no row.
  - Concurrency: 20 parallel first requests for one reservation give exactly one
    Created and 19 Duplicate. I ran it five more times, all green.

  Test check: I broke the store six ways and all were caught: never bumping a
  newer version, treating equal versions as out of date, ignoring price, writing
  on an older duplicate, comparing price as text, and removing the out-of-date
  check.

  One finding: the provider stores 450.00 as the text "450.0". The value is
  exact, but trailing zeros aren't kept. It doesn't affect behavior because
  prices are compared as numbers. My first test run failed on this, and I
  corrected the test helper to read prices numerically. I noted it in plan.md.

  Do you accept chunk 5? If so I'll commit and push, then describe chunk 6, the
  stats store and orchestrator, with the DB clock and future-timestamp
  validation.

● Chunk 5 is committed and pushed as 51f39c3, and the tree is clean. Here is
  chunk 6, with one proposal: it's big enough that I'd split it.

  Proposed split of chunk 6

  The current chunk 6 now holds five things: the DB clock, the future-timestamp
  policy, the stats store, the orchestrator, and its failure handling. I'd
  review them in two steps. Everything after it shifts by one number: HTTP
  endpoints become chunk 8, concurrency tests 9, the cleanup job 10 and wrap-up
  11.

  Chunk 6: building blocks

  - DbClock.NowMs(connection, transaction): the clock query currently private to
    ThrottleStore, moved here so there's one definition. ThrottleStore is
    switched over.
  - UpdatedAtPolicy: a pure class that takes updatedAtUtc and the DB's now, and
    returns an error message if the timestamp is too far ahead, otherwise
    nothing. It's the orchestrator-only validation you described.
    - The allowed skew comes from config (UpdatedAt:MaxFutureSkewSeconds,
      default 300).
    - The message includes the configured value.
    - Negative values fail at startup.
  - StatsStore:
    - Increment(connection, transaction, supplierId, counter) for Ingested,
      Ignored, Invalid and Throttled. It creates the stats row on first use and
      bumps only the chosen column. Each counter maps to a fixed SQL statement,
      with no string-built SQL.
    - Get(connection, supplierId) returns the four counters, or zeros for an
      unknown supplier.
  - Tests:
    - Each counter hits the right column, and others stay untouched.
    - Increments are independent per supplier.
    - The first increment creates the row.
    - The clock is close to the process clock and never goes backwards.
    - The policy passes at exactly the skew and fails one millisecond over, with
      the right message.
    - Config binding is checked through the app.

  Chunk 7: ingest orchestrator (service, no HTTP)

  Ingest(string? body) returns a result the HTTP layer will map in chunk 8:

  ┌───────────────────────────────────┬─────────────────────────────────────┐
  │              Result               │               Meaning               │
  ├───────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Unattributable(errors)            │ 400, no DB access at all            │
  ├───────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Invalid(errors)                   │ admitted, but a bad payload or      │
  │                                   │ future timestamp                    │
  ├───────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Throttled(retryAfterMs, limit,    │ 429                                 │
  │ windowSeconds)                    │                                     │
  ├───────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Processed(status, detail)         │ ingested or ignored, with created,  │
  │                                   │ updated, duplicate or outofdate     │
  ├───────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Failed                            │ 500, after the savepoint rollback   │
  └───────────────────────────────────┴─────────────────────────────────────┘

  The flow runs inside one immediate transaction and one savepoint:
  1. Parse and validate, purely. If unattributable, return without touching the
     DB.
  2. Open the connection and transaction. For a valid request, read the DB
     clock, run the policy, and turn it into Invalid if it objects.
  3. Throttle. If rejected, increment throttled and commit.
  4. If admitted, do all remaining work inside the savepoint. For an invalid
     request that's the invalid increment. For a valid one it's the reservation
     apply plus the ingested or ignored increment.
  5. If that work throws, roll back to the savepoint, keep the admit row,
     commit, log the exception and return Failed.

  Putting the invalid increment inside the savepoint too means even a broken
  stats table can't let a supplier retry unthrottled.

  - Tests, written first, with a real DB: one per request type, checking the
    result, the reservation rows, the request_log rows and every counter. They
    cover:
    - Invalid and future-dated requests count toward the limit.
    - A throttled request writes no reservation, even with a valid payload, and
      a throttled invalid payload counts as throttled, not invalid.
    - A mixed sequence where the four counters add up to the number of
      attributable requests.
  - Failure test: a DB trigger makes inserts into reservations fail. The request
    returns Failed, leaves one extra request_log row, no reservation and no
    counter change. After the trigger is dropped, the next request works. This
    needs no test-only seam in production code.

  Choices I'm making by default: the result shape above, a logged exception on
  Failed, the 300-second future skew, and the savepoint covering all post-admit
  work. I haven't confirmed that the SQLite provider has a savepoint API. If
  not, I'll use plain SAVEPOINT SQL.

  Do you want the split into chunks 6 and 7, or one chunk? And should I start
  chunk 6?

  orchestrator should pass the results to the stats updater, you can start with

● Agreed on the split. On stats, the throttle store already doesn't touch them
  (that was the chunk 4 contract), but your point sharpens the stats side. The
  stats updater should receive the results and own the mapping from result to
  counter, so the orchestrator never picks counters itself. That means
  StatsStore.Record(…) overloads that take the result objects:
  - IngestDetail, which maps to ingested or ignored.
  - ThrottleDecision.Throttled, which maps to throttled.
  - IngestParseResult.Invalid, which maps to invalid.

  No new enum is needed. I'll update the plan first, then start test-first.

● Chunk 6 is implemented and all 109 tests pass. Nothing is committed yet,
  awaiting your acceptance.

  Stats ownership: this follows your point. StatsStore.Record(…) has three
  overloads that take the actual results: an IngestDetail, a
  ThrottleDecision.Throttled, and an IngestParseResult.Invalid. It alone decides
  the counter.
  - Created and updated go to ingested.
  - Duplicate and out-of-date go to ignored.
  - Invalid goes to invalid.
  - Throttled goes to throttled.
  - An unknown detail value throws instead of going uncounted.

  The orchestrator will just pass results along. The throttle and reservation
  stores still never touch stats. Get returns zeros for an unknown supplier.

  Other pieces:
  - DbClock.NowMs: the clock query moved out of ThrottleStore into one
    definition, and the throttle store now uses it.
  - UpdatedAtPolicy: a pure check that takes an updatedAtUtc and the DB's now
    and returns an error message or nothing. It rejects timestamps beyond the
    allowed skew, 300 seconds by default and configurable under UpdatedAt.
    Negative values fail at startup, and the message carries the configured
    value.
  - IngestFields: the wire field names now live in one shared place, used by
    both the parser and the policy.
  - Program.cs: registers the stats store and the policy.

  Tests: 21 new ones, 109 in total. They cover each result type bumping exactly
  its own counter, accumulation and per-supplier isolation, row creation on
  first use, zeros for unknown suppliers, rollback leaving no counts, the clock
  being close and monotonic, the policy at its boundary, and config binding
  through the app.

  Test check: I broke the code six ways and all were caught: counting created as
  ignored, counting out-of-date as ingested, throttled bumping the wrong
  counter, silently counting an unknown detail value, an exclusive skew
  boundary, and a wrong unit for the skew.

  Plan: plan.md has the new split, with chunks renumbered through 11, and I
  removed the stale "awaiting acceptance" labels from the earlier accepted
  chunks.

  Do you accept chunk 6? If so I'll commit and push, then describe chunk 7, the
  orchestrator.

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

● The duplicated constant

  It's fixed. I removed both MillisecondsPerSecond constants and use
  TimeSpan.FromSeconds(n).TotalMilliseconds in the throttle store and the
  policy. All 109 tests still pass. The only * 1000 left is inside the clock
  SQL, converting SQLite's seconds to milliseconds.

  Stats in a different transaction than the reservation

  Yes, it can be different. The split into stores is about who decides what, and
  it doesn't fix what commits together. That's the orchestrator's choice, and
  the stores already accept whatever connection and transaction they're given.
  So separate transactions are possible. The question is what we'd give up.

  Crash between the two
  Same transaction (current plan): Impossible: both commit or neither does
  Stats in a second transaction after the reservation commits: The reservation
  exists but isn't counted, so stats undercount permanently
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Stats write fails (for example a lock timeout)
  Same transaction (current plan): The whole request rolls back to the savepoint
  Stats in a second transaction after the reservation commits: The request
  already succeeded, so the count is lost unless I add retry logic
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Writer-lock acquisitions per request
  Same transaction (current plan): 1
  Stats in a second transaction after the reservation commits: 2
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  What it buys on SQLite
  Same transaction (current plan): n/a
  Stats in a second transaction after the reservation commits: Nothing. There's
  one writer, so shorter transactions don't add parallelism, and total work is
  the same plus an extra lock acquisition
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  What it buys on a server DB
  Same transaction (current plan): n/a
  Stats in a second transaction after the reservation commits: Real relief.
  Every
  request updates the supplier's counter row, which becomes a hot row under
  concurrency, and moving it out of the request transaction relieves that

  Stats before the reservation commit is also possible, but then a crash
  overcounts instead.

  My recommendation is to keep one transaction for this project, because the
  chosen stack gives no throughput gain from splitting and it costs exactness.
  For a server DB, the better design is to write a cheap event row in the same
  transaction and aggregate the counters asynchronously. That keeps exactness
  without the hot-row contention. I'd put this in the README under "what I'd do
  differently."

  Do you want to keep one transaction, or split the stats write out and accept
  the crash drift? If you keep it, I'll add the README line and wait for your
  acceptance of chunk 6.

      20  ## What I'd do differently with more time
      21  - **Redis (or similar) as the window store** instead of the DB:
           lower latency, atomic sliding window, native expiry, one share
          d clock, no load on the reservations DB. Not used because the a
          ssignment fixes the stack.
      22  - **Replace the in-service cleanup job with one external cleane

          r than once per instance. With Redis this disappears entirely (
          native TTL).
      23 +- **Move the stats counters out of the request transaction on a
         + server DB:** write a cheap event row in the same transaction (
         +still exact after a crash) and aggregate the per-supplier count
         +ers asynchronously, so a hot supplier's counter row isn't updat
         +ed on every request. On SQLite there is a single writer, so spl
         +itting would add a second lock acquisition and allow crash drif
         +t for no gain.

● Savepoints are supported: SqliteTransaction has Save, Rollback(name) and

  Chunk 7: ingest orchestrator (service, no HTTP)

  Goal: IngestOrchestrator.Ingest(string? body) runs the full flow on one
  connection and one immediate transaction, and returns a result the HTTP layer
  will map in chunk 8. It decides nothing itself: it asks each component, then
  passes their results to the stats store.

  Result type, in a new Services/ folder, as a small record hierarchy like the
  other result types:

  ┌────────────────┬──────────────────────────────────────────┬─────────────┐
  │     Result     │                 Carries                  │ Future HTTP │
  │                │                                          │     code    │
  ├────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────┼─────────────┤
  │ Unattributable │ errors                                   │ 400         │
  ├────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────┼─────────────┤
  │ Invalid        │ errors                                   │ 400         │
  ├────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────┼─────────────┤
  │ Throttled      │ the throttle decision, with              │ 429         │
  │                │ retryAfterMs, limit and windowSeconds    │             │
  ├────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────┼─────────────┤
  │ Processed      │ the IngestDetail, with a derived         │ 201 or 200  │
  │                │ IngestStatus                             │             │
  ├────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────┼─────────────┤
  │ Failed         │ nothing                                  │ 500         │
  └────────────────┴──────────────────────────────────────────┴─────────────┘

  Flow:
  1. Parse and validate (pure). If the result is unattributable, return without
     opening a connection.
  2. Open the connection and transaction. For a valid request, read the DB clock
     and ask UpdatedAtPolicy. If it objects, treat the request as Invalid,
     wrapping the policy's message.
  3. Throttle. If throttled, pass the throttled decision to StatsStore.Record,
     commit and return Throttled. A throttled invalid payload therefore counts
     as throttled, not invalid.
  4. If admitted, run the remaining work in a savepoint.
     - For an invalid request, pass the invalid result to the stats store.
     - For a valid one, call ReservationStore.Apply, then pass the returned
       IngestDetail to the stats store.
     - On success, release the savepoint and commit.
  5. If the savepoint work throws, roll back to the savepoint, release it, log
     the exception with the supplier ID, commit so the admit row stays, and
     return Failed.

  Anything thrown outside the savepoint propagates as a plain 500 with the whole
  transaction rolled back. That covers the throttle itself, the clock read, and
  a failed throttled counter write. Nothing was admitted in those cases, so no
  slot needs keeping. If the savepoint rollback itself fails, such as a dead
  connection, it propagates the same way.

  One small refactor: the rule "created and updated are ingested, duplicate and
  out-of-date are ignored" exists in StatsStore and would be needed again for
  Processed. I'd add one IngestDetail.ToStatus() extension, used by both, so the
  rule lives in one place.

  Dependencies: connection factory, ThrottleStore, ReservationStore, StatsStore,
  UpdatedAtPolicy and a logger, registered as a singleton.

  Tests, written first, against a real temp DB, checking the result, the
  reservation rows, the request_log rows and every counter each time.
  - Request types:
    - Unattributable touches no table at all.
    - Created, updated, duplicate and out-of-date each give the right result and
      counter.
    - An invalid payload leaves a request_log row, bumps invalid, and writes no
      reservation.
    - A future-dated timestamp gives the policy's message and counts the same
      way. I'll test it with a margin of a minute either side of the limit,
      since the clock is the DB's.
  - Throttling:
    - With 100 existing rows, a throttled valid request writes no reservation
      and adds no row, and returns the retry and rule fields.
    - A throttled invalid payload counts as throttled.
    - 100 valid requests followed by a 101st give 100 processed and 1 throttled.
    - In a mixed sequence, the four counters add up to the number of
      attributable requests.
  - Failures: triggers inject failures, so production code needs no test-only
    hooks.
    - A trigger making reservation inserts fail gives Failed, one extra
      request_log row, no reservation and no counter change. After the trigger
      is dropped, the next request works.
    - A trigger on the stats table with an Updated request gives Failed, the
      stored reservation unchanged, and the slot kept. This proves the
      reservation and counter really are atomic.
    - A trigger on the stats table with an invalid request gives Failed and the
      slot kept, which proves the invalid path is inside the savepoint too.

  Not in this chunk: HTTP, and the full multi-instance concurrency suite (chunk
  9).

  Defaults I'm choosing: the name IngestOrchestrator, the result shape above,
  logging plus Failed on savepoint failures, and the ToStatus() extension.
  Should I go ahead?

● Chunk 7 is implemented and all 135 tests pass. Nothing is committed yet,
  awaiting your acceptance.

  What I built, as designed:
  - IngestOrchestrator.Ingest(string? body) runs the whole flow on one immediate
    transaction, deciding nothing itself.
    - Parse and validate: the pure step runs first. An unattributable request
      returns before any connection is opened.
    - Future-timestamp check: it uses the DB clock via UpdatedAtPolicy. If it
      objects, a valid request becomes Invalid. This completes the verdict
      before the throttle.
    - Throttle: if rejected, the throttled result goes to the stats store, the
      transaction commits, and the caller gets Throttled.
    - Savepoint: everything after admission runs inside it.
    - Failure handling: if that work throws, it rolls back to the savepoint and
      releases it. It then logs the exception with the supplier ID, commits so
      the admit row stays, and returns Failed.
    - Outside the savepoint: the throttle call, the clock read and the throttled
      counter write. An exception there propagates as a plain 500 with the
      transaction rolled back, because nothing was admitted.
  - IngestResult is a small record hierarchy: Unattributable, Invalid,
    Throttled, Processed and Failed. Processed derives its status from the
    detail.
  - IngestDetail.ToStatus(): the created/updated → ingested and
    duplicate/out-of-date → ignored rule now lives in this one extension.
    StatsStore and Processed both use it.

  Tests: 26 new, 135 in total.
  - Request types: unattributable touches no table, and each of created,
    updated, duplicate and out-of-date gives the right result and counters.
  - Invalid requests: an invalid payload and a far-future timestamp each count
    toward the limit and as invalid, with no reservation.
  - Throttling:
    - A throttled request writes nothing and reports the rule.
    - A throttled invalid payload counts as throttled.
    - The 101st request is throttled.
    - Suppliers are independent.
  - Mixed sequence: the counters add up to the attributable requests.
  - Failure injection: DB triggers cause the failures, so production code has no
    test hooks.
    - A failing reservation insert keeps the slot, with no reservation and no
      counters.
    - The next request after that works.
    - A failing stats write on an update rolls the reservation change back, so
      data and stats can't drift.
    - A failing stats write on an invalid request keeps the slot.

  Test check: I broke the orchestrator seven ways and all were caught: no
  savepoint rollback, rethrowing instead of keeping the slot, skipping the
  future check, skipping the throttle for invalid payloads, and leaving out each
  of the invalid, throttled and applied counters. The savepoint-rollback test
  is the only one that catches the first break, and it's the one proving the
  atomicity decision.

  Not covered here: real multi-instance contention and the HTTP layer. Chunks 8
  and 9 cover those.

  Do you accept chunk 7? If so I'll commit and push, then describe chunk 8, the
  HTTP endpoints.

✻ Sautéed for 4m 29s · done Thursday 12:26 AM

※ recap: We're building the supplier feed throttle and dedup service, and chunk 7, the ingest orchestrator, is done with 135 tests passing but uncommitted. Next: you accept it, then I commit, push, and describe chunk 8, the HTTP endpoints.

❯ the return in the throttled looks like a bug, you miss the updating invalid   
  when an invalid comes and gets throlled                                       

● This isn't a bug against what we agreed. I described that behavior when I
  defined the chunk, and you approved it: a throttled invalid payload counts as
  throttled only, so the four counters partition the traffic. But your point
  shows a real cost of that choice. A supplier flooding us with garbage would
  show up as throttled only, and its invalid payloads would vanish from the
  invalid counter exactly when they matter most.

  Your model is cleaner: validity and the throttle outcome are two separate
  facts about a request, and each gets recorded. I'll change it, and the
  consequence is that the counters no longer partition traffic, since a
  throttled invalid request is counted twice, in invalid and in throttled. Tests
  first.

● Update(plan.md)
  ⎿  Added 1 line, removed 1 line
      16  ## Processing order for every ingest request
      17  1. **Parse and validate (pure, in memory, no DB):** `IngestPars
          er.Parse` reads the `supplierId` and validates the payload in o
          ne pass. Result: Unattributable (no usable `supplierId`: 400, n
          othing counted, no DB access), Invalid(supplierId), or Valid.
      18  2. **Future-timestamp validation (orchestrator, needs the DB cl
          ock):** a validation step of its own that belongs to the orches
          trator, not to the reservation. Right after step 1, for a Valid
           request, `updatedAtUtc` more than the configured skew ahead of
           DB time turns it into Invalid(supplierId). It completes the va
          lidity verdict before the throttle.
      19 -3. **Throttle (DB):** atomic admit/reject for Invalid and Valid
         - requests. Rejected -> 429, `throttled`+1, even if the payload 
         -was invalid (it was never admitted).                           
      19 +3. **Throttle (DB):** atomic admit/reject for Invalid and Valid
         + requests. Rejected -> 429, `throttled`+1; if the payload was a
         +lso invalid, `invalid`+1 as well (the verdict is already known 
         +and is recorded for observability, so a supplier sending garbag
         +e stays visible while it is throttled).                        
      20  4. **If admitted and Invalid:** 400, `invalid`+1, written in th
          e same transaction as the admit (the verdict is already known),
           so no second transaction.
      21  5. **If admitted and Valid:** upsert the reservation and classi
          fy the outcome (`ReservationStore`).
      22

● Update(README.md)
  ⎿  Added 1 line, removed 1 line
      10  - **Identity** is `(supplierId, reservationId)`; `updatedAtUtc`
           is the version. An older version with different details is ign
          ored as `outofdate`.
      11  - **Throttle runs before validation**; every attributable reque
          st counts, throttled ones don't occupy the window.
      12  - **One transaction per request**, owned by the orchestrator: t
          he throttle slot, reservation write and stats counter commit to
          gether, so stats can't drift after a crash. The apply step runs
           in a savepoint so a failing request still consumes its quota s
          lot.
      13 -- **Responses and stats share one vocabulary:** `ingested` (cre
         -ated/updated), `ignored` (duplicate/outofdate), `invalid` (400)
         -, `throttled` (429 + `Retry-After`). The counters add up to a s
         -upplier's total traffic; invalid requests count toward the limi
         -t but never touch `reservations`.                              
      13 +- **Responses and stats share one vocabulary:** `ingested` (cre
         +ated/updated), `ignored` (duplicate/outofdate), `invalid` (400)
         +, `throttled` (429 + `Retry-After`). `invalid` counts every bad
         + payload even when the request is also throttled (so a supplier
         + sending garbage stays visible during a flood); invalid request
         +s count toward the limit but never touch `reservations`.       
      14  
      15  ## Where I disagreed with / changed Claude Code's suggestions
      16  - Claude proposed per-instance clocks with accepted skew. Rejec
          ted: it breaks the single-source-of-truth and exact-limit assum
          ptions. Switched to DB-stamped time.

● A throttled invalid request now counts in both invalid and throttled, and all
  138 tests pass. Nothing is committed yet.

  Code: in the throttled branch, the orchestrator now also passes the invalid
  verdict to the stats store when there is one. Both counters are written in the
  same transaction as the throttle. Throttled requests still add no window row
  and no reservation. The same goes for a far-future updatedAtUtc, which also
  counts as invalid. A throttled valid request still counts as throttled only.

  Tests: four cover this.
  - A throttled invalid payload counts as both.
  - A throttled far-future payload counts as both.
  - A throttled valid payload counts as throttled only.
  - Across a mixed sequence, invalid counts every bad payload, throttled or not.

  Test check: two mutations were caught. Dropping the extra invalid recording
  fails three tests, and counting a throttled valid request as invalid fails
  four.

  Docs: plan.md and the README claimed the counters add up to total traffic, so
  I corrected both. The stats section now defines each counter on its own terms.
  ingested + ignored is requests applied, throttled is every 429, and invalid
  is every bad payload, with a throttled invalid request in both. The throttle
  row in the request-types table and the chunk 7 description are updated too.

  One consequence to be aware of: the four counters no longer sum to total
  traffic, so you can't derive a supplier's total request count by adding them.
  If you later want a total, the event-row idea already in the README's "do
  differently" section gives per-request outcomes that can be summed.

  This is the third counting rule we've tried for this case, so I've put the
  reasoning in the code comment and the plan, to keep it from being reversed by
  accident again.

  Do you accept chunk 7? If so I'll commit and push, then describe chunk 8, the
  HTTP endpoints.

✻ Churned for 2m 19s · done Thursday 12:43 AM

❯ chunk 7 approved                                                              

  Pushed to main (ctrl+o to expand)

● Chunk 7 is committed and pushed as c377ad9, and the tree is clean. Chunk 8 is
  described below before I implement it.

  Chunk 8: HTTP endpoints

  Goal: expose the orchestrator and stats over HTTP, with the exact response
  contract from plan.md. The controller only translates; all decisions stay
  below it.

  The trap from the plan: the framework's automatic model binding and validation
  run before our code. A bad body would be rejected with a 400 before the
  throttle ever saw the supplier, which would leave the request uncounted. So
  the ingest action takes no body parameter, reads the raw body itself, and
  passes the string to IngestOrchestrator. I'd skip the [ApiController]
  attribute and use a plain ControllerBase, so nothing automatic can interfere.
  The request Content-Type is ignored: a valid JSON body is accepted whatever
  the header says.

  POST /api/reservations/ingest maps each IngestResult:

  ┌────────────────┬─────┬──────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │     Result     │ HTT │                       Body                       │
  │                │  P  │                                                  │
  ├────────────────┼─────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Processed(Crea │ 201 │ {"status":"ingested","detail":"created"}         │
  │ ted)           │     │                                                  │
  ├────────────────┼─────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Processed(Upda │ 200 │ {"status":"ingested","detail":"updated"}         │
  │ ted)           │     │                                                  │
  ├────────────────┼─────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Processed(Dupl │ 200 │ {"status":"ignored","detail":"duplicate"}        │
  │ icate)         │     │                                                  │
  ├────────────────┼─────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Processed(OutO │ 200 │ {"status":"ignored","detail":"outofdate"}        │
  │ fDate)         │     │                                                  │
  ├────────────────┼─────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Invalid and    │ 400 │ {"status":"invalid","errors":[...]}              │
  │ Unattributable │     │                                                  │
  ├────────────────┼─────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Throttled      │ 429 │ {"status":"throttled","retryAfterMs":…,"limit":… │
  │                │     │ ,"windowSeconds":…}                              │
  ├────────────────┼─────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Failed         │ 500 │ a standard problem-details body                  │
  └────────────────┴─────┴──────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  - Retry-After header on a 429: the exact retry time rounded up to whole
    seconds, because the header can't carry fractions. The body keeps the exact
    milliseconds.
  - Responses use JsonResult with IngestJson.Options: so camelCase, lowercase
    enum words and readable >= come out right without configuring MVC a second
    time.

  GET /api/reservations/stats/{supplierId} returns {supplierId, ingested, 
  ignored, invalid, throttled}, or zeros with a 200 for an unknown supplier. The
  endpoint is not throttled and not counted, since the limit applies to ingest.
  It needs a small StatsService that opens the connection and calls
  StatsStore.Get, so the controller never touches the database.

  Tests, written first, through WebApplicationFactory against a temp DB:
  - Each outcome: the exact status code and the exact JSON text. That covers the
    201 versus 200 split, all four reservation outcomes, and the 400 body for a
    bad payload.
  - Unattributable bodies: an empty body, malformed JSON, a JSON array and a
    blank supplierId all get 400 and touch no table. A valid body sent as
    text/plain still works.
  - 429:
    - I'll configure Throttle:Limit=2, so the third request is rejected without
      inserting 100 rows.
    - The Retry-After header is a whole number of at least 1 and equals the
      body's retryAfterMs rounded up.
    - The body reports limit:2 and windowSeconds:60, proving the configuration
      reaches the response.
  - 500: a DB trigger makes reservation inserts fail. The response is 500 and
    the request still consumes its slot.
  - Stats:
    - They are zero for an unknown supplier.
    - After a mixed sequence, they match the counters.
    - 200 stats reads change nothing, and add no window rows.
  - Routing: a GET to ingest and a POST to stats get 405.

  Defaults I'm choosing:
  - No [ApiController], with the raw body read manually.
  - The Content-Type is ignored.
  - An unattributable request gets the same 400 body shape as an invalid one.
  - A 500 uses standard problem details.
  - Stats reads are unthrottled.
  - I'm not adding a request-size cap. Kestrel's 30 MB default applies, and I'd
    note it as a limitation in the README rather than build hardening the brief
    says isn't expected.
  - The controller calls the orchestrator synchronously. SQLite is in-process,
    so there's nothing to await, but a stalled lock could hold a thread for up
    to the 15-second timeout. That goes in the README as a known limit.

  Should I go ahead with chunk 8?

● Chunk 8 is implemented and all 156 tests pass. Nothing is committed yet,
  awaiting your acceptance. I also ran the real server and exercised it with
  curl.

  What I built:
  - ReservationsController: a plain ControllerBase, deliberately without
    [ApiController]. The ingest action has no body parameter and reads the raw
    body itself, so the framework can't answer a bad body with a 400 before the
    throttle runs. The Content-Type is ignored.
    - POST /api/reservations/ingest maps each result as designed. Created
      returns 201, the other three outcomes return 200, invalid and
      unattributable return 400, throttled returns 429, and failure returns 500.
      A throttled invalid request returns 429, as you specified.
    - On a 429, Retry-After is the exact time rounded up to whole seconds. The
      body carries the exact retryAfterMs, limit and windowSeconds.
    - Responses use JsonResult with IngestJson.Options, so the JSON shape
      matches the earlier tests exactly.
    - GET /api/reservations/stats/{supplierId} returns the four counters, or
      zeros for an unknown supplier. It's neither throttled nor counted.
  - StatsService: a small class that opens a connection and calls
    StatsStore.Get, so the controller never touches the database.

  Tests: 18 new, 156 in total. They cover:
  - the exact status and exact JSON for every outcome;
  - four kinds of bad body, with no table touched;
  - a text/plain body still working;
  - a 429 with the configured limit, a header that equals the body's retry time
    rounded up, and an invalid-but-throttled request returning 429 with
    invalid:1, throttled:1 in stats;
  - a 500 that still consumes its slot;
  - the stats cases, including that reads are neither throttled nor counted;
  - a supplier ID with a space, and 405 for the wrong HTTP methods.

  Test check: I broke the controller six ways and all were caught: always
  returning 201, 429 mapped to 400, failure returning 200, rounding the header
  down, switching to framework body binding (the trap), and ignoring the JSON
  options.

  Real-server check: with the limit set to 3, I sent real requests to the
  running app.
  - The create returned 201 and the resend returned 200 ignored.
  - A bad payload returned 400.
  - A bad payload over the limit returned 429 with Retry-After: 60 and
    retryAfterMs: 59785.
  - A valid payload over the limit also returned 429, and garbage with no
    supplier returned 400.
  - Stats read ingested:1, ignored:1, invalid:2, throttled:2.

  Two mistakes of mine during the real run, both fixed:
  - I first gave the app a Git Bash style path that .NET can't read. It failed
    fast with a clear SQLite error.
  - I reported the server "stopped" when it hadn't, because pgrep and pkill
    aren't installed here. The busy DB file exposed it, so I found the process
    by its command line, stopped it, and confirmed no smoke-test server remains.

  The repo has no stray files. Do you accept chunk 8? If so I'll commit and
  push, then describe chunk 9, the multi-instance concurrency tests.

  older than the window about once a minute, with a configurable interval.
  - Test: it must delete only expired rows and never change a throttle decision.
  - Known compromise: it runs inside every instance, which the README already
    marks as a disagreement. An external cleaner is the better design.

    I'd cut it back to key decisions plus the three required sections.
  - Known limitations to record: these belong in the README or plan.md.
    - The controller calls the orchestrator synchronously, so a stalled lock can
      hold a thread for up to the 15-second timeout.
    - There is no request-size cap.
    - The SQLite clock is the host's.
