● The test output folder already holds a complete, runnable copy of the API
  (.dll, .deps.json and .runtimeconfig.json). So a test can start real server
  processes from it, with no path guessing. Here is chunk 9.

  Chunk 9: concurrency tests

  Goal: prove the headline requirement, that the limit and the data stay correct
  under real contention, across instances. Everything so far was tested one
  request at a time, apart from small parallel checks on the individual stores.
  This chunk is mostly tests, and I expect little or no production code, unless
  it finds a bug.

  Three layers, each stronger than the last:

  1. Orchestrator under parallel load, in-process with separate connections:
     - 150 parallel valid requests for one supplier give exactly 100 Processed
       and 50 Throttled. Reservations number 100, the window holds 100 rows, and
       the counters read ingested:100, throttled:50.
     - Three suppliers sent 120 requests each, all at once, give exactly 100
       admitted and 20 throttled for each. This shows no interference under
       contention.
     - 50 concurrent versions of one reservation, with increasing updatedAtUtc
       and different details, sent in random order. The final stored row must be
       the newest version, never an older one that happened to run last. This
       is the "concurrent writers can't regress data" requirement. The counts
       must also add up.
  2. Several app hosts sharing one DB file, in one process. Three
     WebApplicationFactory hosts each run their own startup and schema init
     against the same file, and 150 HTTP requests are fired across them at once.
     The result is exactly 100 201, 50 429, no 500, and stats read from any
     host agree.
  3. Real separate OS processes: two actual server processes started from the
     test output, on ephemeral ports whose numbers I read from each server's
     "Now listening on" line, sharing one DB file. 150 requests go to both at
     once. This covers the case the spike left open: real file locking between
     processes, not just threads. I'd put it in a test category, MultiProcess,
     so it can be excluded with --filter "TestCategory!=MultiProcess". It's
     slower (about a second or two per process start), and the cleanup must
     always kill the processes.

  What these catch: over-admission, a 500 caused by lock contention, lost or
  regressed updates, and counters that drift from the rows.

  Repeatability: the in-process tests use NUnit's [Repeat], and I'll also run
  the whole suite ten times by hand and report any flake.

  Test check: I'll check the tests can actually fail. One way is to switch the
  orchestrator's transaction to a deferred one, which the spike showed produces
  "database is locked" errors. Another is to remove the lock-holding behavior.
  Both should turn these tests red.

  Not covered, and said plainly: true multiple hosts. All instances here share
  one machine and one clock, and SQLite can't safely span machines anyway. That
  stays a documented limitation.

  Should I go ahead with chunk 9 as described?

      73  ## Known limitations
      74  - **SQLite clock:** SQLite has no server clock. Its "now" is th
          e OS clock of the app process, so "DB as single clock" holds on
          ly because all instances share one host. Multi-host needs a ser
          ver DB (e.g. SQL Server `SYSUTCDATETIME()` with `UPDLOCK, HOLDL
          OCK`). Instances on different hosts can't safely share a SQLite
           file over a network filesystem.
      75  - No monotonic time in SQLite: NTP/manual clock jumps on the ho
          st shift the window.
      76 -- **Single writer lock:** every request writes (log row, counte
         -rs; throttled ones too), so a flooding supplier can slow others
         -.                                                              
      76 +- **Single writer lock:** every request writes (log row, counte
         +rs; throttled ones too), so a flooding supplier can slow others
         +. Measured, see "Measured locking behavior": throughput falls a
         +s callers pile up, and scaling out instances does not add write
         + throughput.                                                   
      77  - **Unattributable garbage** is unthrottled; without auth, anyo
          ne can spoof a `supplierId` and burn its quota. A per-IP limit 
          would be the real fix (out of scope).
      78  - "Multiple instances" in tests are parallel connections in one
           process: same clock, not proof of multi-host behavior.

      107 +An on-demand report (`dotnet test -c Release --filter "TestCat
          +egory=Performance" --logger "console;verbosity=detailed"`, tes
          +t class `LockingPerformanceReport`) measures the single writer
          + lock. Release build, 8 cores, one local disk, 300 requests pe
          +r row, callers on dedicated threads, full write path (parse, t
          +hrottle, reservation, stats, commit). Reproducible within abou
          +t 10% across runs.                                            
      108 +                                                              
      109 +| Scenario | Callers | req/s | p50 ms | p99 ms | max ms |     
      110 +|---|---|---|---|---|---|                                     
      111 +| New reservation per request, never throttled | 1 | 355 | 2.6
          + | 5.1 | 38 |                                                 
      112 +| | 8 | 170 | 2.6 | 1284 | 1763 |                             
      113 +| | 32 | 54 | 2.8 | 5116 | 5593 |                             
      114 +| One supplier already throttled (abuse path) | 1 | 423 | 2.3 
          +| 3.8 | 6 |                                                   
      115 +| | 32 | 83 | 2.3 | 3138 | 3610 |                             
      116 +| One supplier flooding from empty | 1 | 394 | 2.4 | 3.8 | 14 
          +|                                                             
      117 +| | 32 | 59 | 2.5 | 4597 | 4935 |                             
      118 +| Same, 2 real server processes over HTTP | 8 | 140 | 3.0 | 14
          +28 | 2143 |                                                   
      119 +| | 32 | 107 | 80.8 | 2315 | 2793 |

          + of the DB) | 8 / 16 / 32 | 382 / 377 / 373 | 2.6 | ~770-790 |
          + ~780-800 |                                                   
      121 +                                                              
      122 +What it shows:                                                
      123 +- **A request that gets the lock is fast (p50 ~2.5ms); the cos
          +t is waiting for it.** Throughput falls as callers are added (
          +355 req/s with 1 caller, 54 with 32) and the tail explodes (p9
          +9 over 5s at 32 callers). The writer lock is the bottleneck, a
          +nd waiting callers make it worse, not better.                 
      124 +- **The throttled (abuse) path costs about the same as a norma
          +l write** (it still commits a counter), so a flooding supplier
          + occupies the shared lock as much as normal traffic and raises
          + latency for every supplier.                                  
      125 +- **Scaling out does not help write throughput:** two real pro
          +cesses sharing the file were no faster than one. All instances
          + contend for the same file lock.                              
      126 +- **The cause is the waiting, not the disk:** with 8 callers, 
          +'synchronous = NORMAL' did not raise commit throughput (264-59
          +8/s versus 230-306/s for the default FULL), although it made s
          +ingle-caller commits 15-20x faster (about 6000-9000/s versus 4
          +10-450/s).                                                    
      127 +- **A queue in front of the lock fixes the collapse:** letting
          + 1 writer per process into the database at a time (the rest wa
          +it in memory) held throughput at the single-caller level (~375
          + req/s) for 8, 16 and 32 callers, finished the 300 requests in
          + 0.8s instead of 5.1s, and cut the worst wait from 4.9s to 0.8
          +s. Not implemented yet (a design decision); the experiment is 
          +in the report (section D).                                    
      128 +- **Likely next steps, in order of payoff:** bound concurrent 
          +writers per instance (about 6x at 32 callers); then `synchrono
          +us = NORMAL` once writers are serialized (trade-off: after an 
          +OS or power failure the last few commits may be lost, which ca
          +n let a supplier slightly exceed its limit once; an applicatio
          +n crash alone loses nothing; this combination is unmeasured en
          +d to end); for real scale, Redis for the window (README).     
      129 +

       6  
       7  ## Key decisions / assumptions
       8  - **Exact limit, no slack:** request 101 in any rolling 60s is 
          never admitted. The cost is DB writes per request, a deliberate
           trade of performance for accuracy.
       9 -- **The database is the single source of truth** for the window
         - state and the clock (one atomic check-and-insert). On SQLite t
         -his only holds on one host: SQLite's "now" is the app process's
         - OS clock.                                                     
       9 +- **The database is the single source of truth** for the window
         + state and the clock (one atomic check-and-insert). On SQLite t
         +his only holds on one host: SQLite's "now" is the app process's
         + OS clock. Multi-instance behavior is tested with real separate
         + processes sharing one file on one machine; different-machine b
         +ehavior is out of scope because SQLite cannot span machines.   
      10  - **Identity** is `(supplierId, reservationId)`; `updatedAtUtc`
           is the version. An older version with different details is ign
          ored as `outofdate`.
      11  - **Throttle runs before validation**; every attributable reque
          st counts, throttled ones don't occupy the window.
      12  - **One transaction per request**, owned by the orchestrator: t
          he throttle slot, reservation write and stats counter commit to
          gether, so stats can't drift after a crash. The apply step runs
           in a savepoint so a failing request still consumes its quota s
          lot.

      20  ## What I'd do differently with more time
      21  - **Redis (or similar) as the window store** instead of the DB:
           lower latency, atomic sliding window, native expiry, one share
          d clock, no load on the reservations DB. Not used because the a
          ssignment fixes the stack.
      22  - **Replace the in-service cleanup job with one external cleane
          r** (cron job or DB-side scheduler), so cleanup runs once rathe
          r than once per instance. With Redis this disappears entirely (
          native TTL).
      23 +- **Bound concurrent writers per instance.** Measured: SQLite h
         +as one writer, so throughput falls from ~355 req/s with 1 calle
         +r to ~54 with 32 (p99 over 5s), and a second server process add
         +s nothing. A queue in front of the DB held ~375 req/s at 32 cal
         +lers (6x) in an experiment; not implemented. Details in `plan.m
         +d`.                                                            
      24  - **Move the stats counters out of the request transaction on a
           server DB:** write a cheap event row in the same transaction (
          still exact after a crash) and aggregate the per-supplier count
          ers asynchronously, so a hot supplier's counter row isn't updat
          ed on every request. On SQLite there is a single writer, so spl
          itting would add a second lock acquisition and allow crash drif
          t for no gain.
      25  - A server DB (e.g. SQL Server) with proper locking, per-suppli
          er auth, per-IP limits, windowed stats.

● Chunk 9 is implemented and all 165 tests pass. A plain dotnet test skips the
  performance report. Nothing is committed yet, awaiting your acceptance.

  Concurrency and multi-instance tests

  - Orchestrator under load: 16 callers, each on its own connection.
    - 150 requests from one supplier give exactly 100 processed and 50
      throttled.
    - Three suppliers sent 120 requests each all get exactly 100 admitted and 20
      throttled.
    - 50 concurrent versions of one reservation, in random order, always leave
      the newest version stored and create it exactly once.
    - A mixed valid and invalid load keeps every counter consistent.
  - Three hosts sharing one DB file: exactly 100 201 and 50 429, no 500, and the
    stats agree from any host.
  - Two real server processes started at the same moment: the same limit and
    version tests. This also covers concurrent schema creation, and it works. It
    sits under Category("MultiProcess").
  - Flakiness: I repeated the suite three times with all tests green.

  What the tests caught about themselves

  - Surviving mutation: switching the orchestrator to a deferred transaction
    survived the load tests. The unsafe window is microseconds wide, so load
    rarely hits it.
  - Fix: I added BeginWriteTransaction, which the orchestrator now calls. The
    lock test pins that exact method, and the deferred mutation now fails it.
  - Two other mutations caught: letting older versions overwrite newer ones, and
    an off-by-one limit.

  Locking performance (Release, this machine)

  Details are in plan.md and the on-demand report, LockingPerformanceReport.

  ┌─────────────────────────────┬───────────┬────────────────────┐
  │          Scenario           │ 1 caller  │     32 callers     │
  ├─────────────────────────────┼───────────┼────────────────────┤
  │ New reservation per request │ 355 req/s │ 54 req/s, p99 5.1s │
  ├─────────────────────────────┼───────────┼────────────────────┤
  │ Throttled abuse path        │ 423 req/s │ 83 req/s, p99 3.1s │
  ├─────────────────────────────┼───────────┼────────────────────┤
  │ Flood from one supplier     │ 394 req/s │ 59 req/s, p99 4.6s │
  └─────────────────────────────┴───────────┴────────────────────┘

  - Waiting for the lock is the cost. A request that gets the lock takes about
    2.5ms at any load. Throughput falls as callers are added, and more callers
    make it worse.
  - Abuse costs as much as normal traffic. The throttled path commits a counter,
    so a flooding supplier raises latency for every supplier.
  - Scaling out doesn't help. Two real processes were no faster than one (107
    req/s versus 151 at 32 callers).
  - The cause is the waiting. synchronous = NORMAL made single-caller commits 15
    to 20 times faster, but didn't help at 8 callers.
  - A queue in front of the DB fixes the collapse. In an experiment, letting one
    writer into the database at a time held about 375 req/s at 8, 16 and 32
    callers. That's 6 times faster than 59 req/s without the queue. The 300
    requests finished in 0.8s instead of 5.1s, and the worst wait dropped from
    4.9s to 0.8s.

  I did not add the queue as production code, since it's a design decision. My
  recommendation is to add it, one writer per instance and configurable, as the
  biggest cheap win. synchronous = NORMAL would be a second step, but it can
  lose the last few commits after an OS or power failure. The measured gains are
  in the plan.md limitations and the README's "do differently" list.

  Documented limits

  - Different machines: out of scope, as you said. SQLite cannot span machines.
    It's recorded in plan.md and the README.
  - Lock timeouts: a request waits up to 15s for the lock, then fails with
    a 500. The tests reached about 5s at 32 callers, and failure at higher
    concurrency was not measured.

  Do you accept chunk 9? Should the write queue be part of chunk 9, or its own
  small chunk after it? If you accept chunk 9 as it is, I'll commit and push,
  and describe chunk 10, the cleanup job.

❯ the tests looks good, the locking constraint is expected because of sqllite,  
  it means we are capped becasue of the technology which makes scaling not      
  preform any better, is there a way using sqllite to enchance scaling          
  preformence?                                                                  

● Yes, there are several. SQLite allows one writer per database file, so the
  options fall into two groups: using that one writer better, and having more
  writers by using more files. I'd rather give you measured numbers than
  guesses, so I'll add an experiment for the most promising idea. It exploits a
  property of our data model: every piece of state is keyed by supplier.

● The experiment code is removed, and the repo is back to the state you
  reviewed. I ran nothing and measured nothing new.

  Your design idea

  A separate writer service fed by a message broker is a sound pattern. It's a
  standard way to scale around a single writer. One thing needs deciding before
  it's built: today the supplier gets the throttle verdict in the response. With
  a broker the decision is made later by the writer, so we'd either use request
  and reply to keep the answer synchronous, or answer 202 Accepted and report
  throttling some other way. The second changes the 429 contract. I agree it's
  out of scope now, and I'd record it in the README as the scale-out direction.

  Ways to improve things within SQLite

  Only the first one has been measured, and none are implemented.

  1. Queue writers in memory in front of the lock. Measured: about 6 times the
     throughput at 32 callers, and a worst wait of 0.8s instead of 4.9s. It's
     the cheapest fix and stays inside one process, though instances still
     contend with each other.
  2. synchronous = NORMAL once writers are serialized. Measured only on single
     commits: 15 to 20 times faster. After an OS or power failure, the last few
     commits may be lost.
  3. Split suppliers across several database files. Every piece of state is
     keyed by supplier, so a supplier can live entirely in one file, and each
     file has its own writer lock.
     - A request touches one supplier, so it still touches one file and keeps
       its single transaction.
     - A flooding supplier would only slow the suppliers that share its file.
     - Costs: the number of files must be fixed up front, since changing it
       means moving data, and every file needs its own schema init and cleanup.
     - I expect near-linear scaling until the disk's sync rate is the limit.
       That's a hypothesis, not a measurement.
  4. Batching (group commit). Many requests share one transaction and one disk
     sync, with each request in its own savepoint so a failure stays isolated.
     This could reach thousands of requests per second per file, but it adds
     batching latency and real complexity.
  5. Smaller wins. Fewer statements per request, or cheaper throttled-path
     writes. The throttled path still commits a counter row, but removing it
     would make throttled less exact. This only helps a little.

  What won't help: more instances on one file. That's measured: two processes
  were no faster than one.

  My suggested order, if you decide to do any of it later, is 1, then 3 if one
  file's capacity isn't enough, then 2. Option 4 is only worth it if the
  database is the proven bottleneck after those.

  I can run experiments for options 1 to 3 whenever you approve them. Chunk 9 is
  unchanged and still awaits your acceptance. If you accept it as it is, I'll
  commit and push, then describe chunk 10.

      20  ## What I'd do differently with more time
      21  - **Redis (or similar) as the window store** instead of the DB:
           lower latency, atomic sliding window, native expiry, one share
          d clock, no load on the reservations DB. Not used because the a
          ssignment fixes the stack.
      22  - **Replace the in-service cleanup job with one external cleane
          r** (cron job or DB-side scheduler), so cleanup runs once rathe
          r than once per instance. With Redis this disappears entirely (
          native TTL).
      23 +- **Scale writes with a dedicated writer service fed by a messa
         +ge broker.** SQLite allows one writer, so the service that writ
         +es would be a single, non-scaled consumer while the API tier sc
         +ales; this moves the throttle verdict off the request path, so 
         +it needs a decision (request-reply, or 202 and report throttlin
         +g another way). Out of scope here.                             
      24  - **Bound concurrent writers per instance.** Measured: SQLite h
          as one writer, so throughput falls from ~355 req/s with 1 calle
          r to ~54 with 32 (p99 over 5s), and a second server process add
          s nothing. A queue in front of the DB held ~375 req/s at 32 cal
          lers (6x) in an experiment; not implemented. Details in `plan.m
          d`.
      25  - **Move the stats counters out of the request transaction on a
           server DB:** write a cheap event row in the same transaction (
          still exact after a crash) and aggregate the per-supplier count
          ers asynchronously, so a hot supplier's counter row isn't updat
          ed on every request. On SQLite there is a single writer, so spl
          itting would add a second lock acquisition and allow crash drif
          t for no gain.

● Chunk 9 is committed and pushed as 184a00f, and the tree is clean. All 165
  tests pass. Chunk 10 is described below before I implement it.

  Chunk 10: cleanup job

  Goal: keep request_log small by deleting rows that have left the throttle
  window. Correctness never depends on it. The count query only reads in-window
  rows, so this is purely about table size. It also covers the rows left by
  quiet or spoofed suppliers.

  Pieces
  - RequestLogCleaner, in the data layer, does one cleanup pass:
    - It reads the DB clock and deletes rows with tsMs <= now - window, using
      the same window as the throttle (ThrottleOptions).
    - That's the exact complement of the throttle's tsMs > now - window, so it
      only ever removes rows no future request can count.
    - It deletes in batches of 1,000 (configurable), each batch in its own short
      write transaction. The measurements showed the writer lock is the scarce
      resource, so a long delete after a downtime backlog must not hold it.
    - It returns how many rows it deleted.
  - RequestLogCleanupService, a BackgroundService, runs the cleaner every
    Cleanup:IntervalSeconds (default 60). Details:
    - Start delay: the first run happens after a random delay within one
      interval, so instances started together don't all clean at the same
      instant.
    - Errors: an exception in a pass is logged and the loop continues. Without
      that, .NET would stop the whole host over a failed delete.
  - Settings: a Cleanup section with IntervalSeconds and BatchSize. Non-positive
    values fail at startup.

  Tests, written first
  - Retention: rows older than the window are deleted and in-window rows are
    kept (margins of 61s and 30s). With a fixed clock, a row exactly one window
    old is deleted, and one 1ms younger is kept.
  - Never changes a throttle decision: for any later instant within the window,
    the count the throttle would see is identical before and after a pass.
  - Batching: 2,500 expired rows with a batch size of 1,000 are all removed and
    the return value is correct.
  - Under load: with thousands of expired rows present, a cleanup loop runs on
    its own thread while 150 parallel requests arrive. Exactly 100 are still
    admitted.
  - The hosted service:
    - With a 1-second interval, expired rows disappear within a few seconds.
    - A forced failure, a trigger that raises on delete, doesn't stop it. After
      the trigger is dropped, cleanup resumes.
  - Config: invalid values are rejected, and the service is wired into the real
    app.

  Not in this chunk: an external cleaner, a cron job or a DB-side scheduler.
  That stays in the README's "do differently" list.

  Choices I'm making by default:
  - Batch size 1,000.
  - A random start delay.
  - Deletion keyed to the throttle's window with no safety margin. That assumes
    the clock never runs backwards, since a backward NTP jump could shrink the
    window, which is the same host-clock caveat already documented.
  - The service runs in every instance and is idempotent.

  Should I go ahead with chunk 10?

● Chunk 10 is implemented and all 186 tests pass. Nothing is committed yet,
  awaiting your acceptance.

  What I built:
  - RequestLogCleaner, in the data layer.
    - One pass deletes rows with tsMs <= now - window, using the DB clock and
      the throttle's own window setting. That's the exact complement of the rule
      the throttle uses to count rows.
    - It works in batches of 1,000. Each batch is its own short write
      transaction, so a big backlog never holds the writer lock for long, and
      the measurements showed that lock is the scarce resource.
  - RequestLogCleanupService, a BackgroundService running the cleaner every
    Cleanup:IntervalSeconds, default 60. The first pass is after a random delay,
    so instances started together don't clean in lockstep. A failed pass is
    logged and retried on the next tick. It doesn't stop the host, which an
    unhandled exception in a background service would.
  - CleanupOptions: interval and batch size, validated at startup, so a bad
    value stops the app from starting.
  - Program.cs: wires everything in.

  Tests: 21 new, 186 in total.
  - What gets deleted:
    - Rows older than the window go and in-window rows stay.
    - A row exactly one window old is deleted, and one 1ms younger is kept.
    - Every supplier's expired rows go in one pass.
    - The window follows the throttle options.
  - Batching: a 2,500-row backlog with a batch size of 1,000 is fully removed.
    So is an exact multiple of the batch size.
  - Never changes a decision: the count the throttle would see at every later
    second within the window is identical before and after a pass.
  - Under load: cleaning continuously in a loop while 150 requests arrive from
    16 callers still admits exactly 100, and the 4,500-row expired backlog is
    gone afterward.
  - The service: it deletes expired rows on its own, picks up rows that expire
    later, survives a forced failure and recovers once the cause is removed, and
    stops promptly.
  - Wiring and config: it's registered in the app, and invalid config stops
    startup.

  Test check: four mutations were caught: an exclusive boundary, deleting
  in-window rows, deleting only the first batch, and not catching a failed pass.
  The deleting-in-window-rows case showed a gap. My first version of that
  mutation only removed rows older than 20 seconds, which the load test never
  touches. With a mutation that deletes live rows, the supplier got 110 requests
  through instead of 100, so the load test does catch the dangerous case.

  Known behavior: each batch scans the table, since an index on tsMs alone would
  slow the hot insert path. The table is bounded at roughly the limit per
  supplier, so a scan is cheap. The README and plan.md already record the
  external cleaner as the better design.

  Do you accept chunk 10? If so I'll commit and push, then describe chunk 11,
  the wrap-up.

※ recap: We're building the supplier feed throttle and dedup service chunk by chunk; chunk 10, the request log cleanup job, is done with all 186 tests passing but uncommitted. Next: say whether you accept chunk 10, and I'll commit, push, and describe the wrap-up.

❯ why did you add the if deleted < _batchsize                                   

● It's the loop's stop condition, and it's there to save a transaction in the
  common case.

  What it does:
  - Each pass deletes expired rows in batches. One batch is DELETE ... LIMIT 
    batchSize, which removes at most batchSize rows.
  - If a batch deleted fewer rows than batchSize, it ran out of expired rows, so
    there's nothing left to do and the pass ends.
  - If it deleted exactly batchSize, there may be more, so it runs another
    batch.

  Why not loop until a batch deletes 0 rows? That also works and is simpler to
  read, but it always costs one extra batch. Passes run every minute in every
  instance. Most passes find a few expired rows, or none, so a "loop until zero"
  version would run a second, empty write transaction on every pass. That empty
  transaction still takes the single writer lock, which is the scarce resource
  in this design. With the short-batch check, the common case is one
  transaction.

  Is stopping early safe? Yes. The batch runs inside a write transaction, so no
  other writer can change request_log while it runs. A batch that deleted fewer
  rows than the limit really did take every expired row. Rows that expire after
  that moment are picked up by the next pass.

  The one cost: if the backlog is an exact multiple of the batch size, such as
  2,000 rows with a batch of 1,000, the loop runs one extra empty batch. That's
  harmless. A test,
  A_backlog_that_is_an_exact_multiple_of_the_batch_size_is_fully_deleted, covers
  it. Another test, Batches_smaller_than_the_backlog_still_delete_everything,
  covers the multi-batch case. In my mutation check, replacing the condition
  with "always stop after the first batch" failed both.

  If you'd prefer the simpler "loop until zero" form for readability, say so and
  I'll change it, at the cost of that extra transaction per pass.

  Chunk 11: wrap-up

  1. Fix what's stale or wrong in the docs
  - README status line: it still says "planning complete, implementation not
    started."
  - README length: it's about 700 words, and the brief says half a page is
    plenty. I'd cut it to roughly 300 words by keeping only decisions that shape
    behavior, plus the two other required sections. The detail stays in
    plan.md.
  - README "throttle runs before validation": this describes the old flow. The
    real flow is pure parse and validate, then the throttle on every
    attributable request, then act on validity.
  - plan.md inaccuracies:
    - It says the reservation upsert is a "conditional UPDATE". It's actually
      read-then-write under the write lock.
    - The price-storage assumption still says "decide at implementation."
    - Some "Next steps" and "Open items" sections predate the build.

  ## Where I disagreed with / changed Claude Code's suggestions
  - **Clock.** Claude proposed each instance using its own clock and tolerating
  a little skew. I chose the database as the only clock, because the limit has
  to be exact.
  - **Cleanup.** Claude proposed deleting old rows on every request, then a
  cleanup job in every instance. I rejected both (extra writes, redundant work)
  and wanted one external cleaner; SQLite has no expiry or scheduler, so the
  in-service job stays as a compromise.
  - **Retry time.** Claude rounded it to whole seconds and could return 0 to a
  throttled caller, which invites an instant retry. I flagged the bad UX; fixing
  it exposed a race, so the time is now exact and always above zero (only the
  HTTP header rounds up, as HTTP requires).
  - **Stats.** Claude kept duplicates and out-of-date updates as separate
  counters. I merged them into one "ignored" bucket, made responses use the same
  words as the stats, and added an "invalid" counter (a throttled invalid
  request counts in both, for visibility).

  Rules I followed:
  - No class, file or method names.
  - No jargon like "savepoint" or "orchestrator".
  - Each bullet follows the same pattern: what Claude proposed, what you chose,
    and why.

  Length:
  - The section is about 140 words. The README overall is still 773 words
    against a target of about 300, so the other sections need the real cuts.
  - If you want this section tighter, the cleanup and stats bullets are the
    longest.

  Two items you didn't mention:
  - Transactions (#2): I left it out of the new section. That decision was
    partly me reconsidering and partly your design. It's also still described in
    "Key decisions", but in implementation terms. You may prefer it as a
    disagreement, or as a plain-language key decision.
  - Scope (#7): the SQLite cap and the experiments you stopped. I'd cover it
    under "What I'd do differently", where it already lives.

  Next: we still have to decide what to keep in "Key decisions / assumptions"
  and "What I'd do differently". Those two hold 490 of the 773 words, and "Key
  decisions" still contains implementation names and the old "throttle before
  validation" wording. Which should we do first, and do you want #2 back in the
  disagreements?

● I rewrote the section and removed the parenthetical sentence. It's saved to
  disk and uncommitted.

  ## Key decisions / assumptions
  - **Exact limit, no slack.** Request 101 in any 60 seconds is never admitted,
  because the spec says "more than 100"; I pay for that with a database write
  per request.
  - **The database is the single source of truth** for the request count and the
  clock. Instances keep no counters or clocks of their own, so the limit stays
  exact across them.
  - **A reservation is identified by supplier and reservation ID, and 
  `updatedAtUtc` is its version.** Suppliers may reuse IDs, and a delayed old
  retry must never overwrite newer data.
  - **Every request counts toward the limit, even invalid ones.** They still
  cost work, and a supplier sending garbage should be limited too. Rejected
  requests don't count, so a supplier is released as soon as old ones age out.
  - **One transaction per request**, so the count, the data and the stats commit
  together and a crash can't leave them disagreeing.
  - **Responses and stats use the same words** (ingested, ignored, invalid,
  throttled), so what a supplier is told matches what we count.

  Changes beyond trimming:
  - Reasons added: every bullet now ends with why.
  - Stale claim fixed: the old text said "Throttle runs before validation",
    which isn't how it works any more. The new bullet says every request counts
    toward the limit, including invalid ones, which is the behavior and the
    reason together.
  - One new bullet: I added "Exact limit, no slack" with its reason. It was
    already in the old text as a trade-off sentence.
  - No code names: the one code-like term is updatedAtUtc. It's a field from the
    assignment's own request format, so I kept it.

  Length: the section went from 218 to about 180 words, which is shorter but not
  by much, because the reasons add words. The README is 735 words in total. If
  you want it tighter, the cheapest cut is merging "Exact limit" into the
  single-source bullet, or dropping the last bullet. I'd keep the transaction
  bullet, since it's a real design decision.

  The remaining big section is "What I'd do differently", at 273 words, which is
  the next place to cut. Do you want to go through that one now?

  ⎿  Removed 1 line
      42  - **Redis (or similar) as the window store** instead of the DB:
           lower latency, atomic sliding window, native expiry, one share
          d clock, no load on the reservations DB. Not used because the a
          ssignment fixes the stack.
      43  - **Replace the in-service cleanup job with one external cleane
          r** (cron job or DB-side scheduler), so cleanup runs once rathe
          r than once per instance. With Redis this disappears entirely (
          native TTL).
      44  - **Scale writes with a dedicated writer service fed by a messa
          ge broker.** SQLite allows one writer, so the service that writ
          es would be a single, non-scaled consumer while the API tier sc
          ales; this moves the throttle verdict off the request path, so 
          it needs a decision (request-reply, or 202 and report throttlin
          g another way). Out of scope here.
      45 -- **Bound concurrent writers per instance.** Measured: SQLite h
         -as one writer, so throughput falls from ~355 req/s with 1 calle
         -r to ~54 with 32 (p99 over 5s), and a second server process add
         -s nothing. A queue in front of the DB held ~375 req/s at 32 cal
         -lers (6x) in an experiment; not implemented. Details in `plan.m
         -d`.                                                            
      45  - **Move the stats counters out of the request transaction on a
           server DB:** write a cheap event row in the same transaction (
          still exact after a crash) and aggregate the per-supplier count
          ers asynchronously, so a hot supplier's counter row isn't updat
          ed on every request. On SQLite there is a single writer, so spl
          itting would add a second lock acquisition and allow crash drif
          t for no gain.
      46  - A server DB (e.g. SQL Server) with proper locking, per-suppli
          er auth, per-IP limits, windowed stats.

● I consolidated "What I'd do differently" from five bullets to two, and it's
  saved to disk, uncommitted. The section went from about 190 words to about 92.

  ## What I'd do differently with more time
  - **Replace SQLite: an external cache such as Redis for the request window, a 
  server database for reservations.** Redis expires old entries by itself (no
  cleanup job) and gives every instance one clock, and a server database allows
  many writers, where SQLite's single writer caps how far the service can scale.
  I didn't because the assignment fixes the stack.
  - **Per-supplier authentication and per-IP limits**, so one caller can't use
  up another supplier's quota and requests that don't name a supplier are
  limited too.

  What I merged and dropped:
  - Redis plus the external cleaner: the cleaner point is now just "no cleanup
    job", folded into the Redis bullet.
  - The message-broker writer service: dropped. It only existed to work around
    SQLite's one-writer limit, as you said, so replacing SQLite removes the
    reason for it.
  - Moving the stats counters out of the request transaction: dropped. It's an
    extension of moving to a server database.
  - "Windowed stats": dropped as a minor extra.
  - Kept as its own point: authentication and per-IP limits. They're not caused
    by SQLite, so they stand apart. Without authentication, anyone can spend
    another supplier's quota.

  One thing worth knowing: the Redis bullet says a server database allows many
  writers, but it doesn't mention that the cleanup job also appears under "Where
  I disagreed". That's fine, since it's a different framing: the disagreement
  is about what Claude proposed, and this is what you'd change. If you think it
  reads as repetition, the cleanup disagreement bullet is the one to consider
  shortening.

  Totals: the README is 549 words, down from 773 at the start of this
  discussion. The target was about 300, and the remaining weight is in two
  places:
  - Key decisions: about 200 words, because of the four response definitions.
  - Run it: about 73 words.

  Do you want to trim those, or is 549 acceptable? The assignment says half a
  page is plenty, so a README at roughly twice that may be fine, but I'd leave
  the call to you.

  ## What I'd do differently with more time
  - **Replace SQLite with a server database.** SQLite allows only one writer at
  a time and can't be shared across machines, which caps how far the service can
  scale. I kept it because the assignment fixes the stack.
  - **Keep the request window in a cache such as Redis, not in the database.**
  It keeps the per-request throttle writes off the database, and every instance
  shares one clock.
  - **Remove the cleanup job**, because the cache expires old entries by itself.
  - **Per-supplier authentication and per-IP limits**, so one caller can't use
  up another supplier's quota and requests that don't name a supplier are
  limited too.

    SQLite.

● The review returned 9 findings and no critical ones. I changed nothing. I read
  the code behind three of them and judged the rest from our earlier decisions,
  so that's marked below.

  Findings, ranked, with my verdict

  #: 1
  Finding: Error handling after a failed apply: the failure handler rolls back
  to
  the savepoint and then logs. If SQLite already aborted the whole transaction
  (disk full, I/O error), the rollback itself throws. The request ends in a
  plain 500 and the original error is never logged.
  My verdict: Real bug, I verified it. The code does roll back and release first

  and log last. It's rare, but the fix is small: log first, and don't let a
  failed rollback hide the original error.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: 2
  Finding: Startup: the schema and WAL switch aren't checked. If the WAL switch
  silently didn't take effect, the database would stay in the default mode,
  which our throttle design doesn't expect.
  My verdict: Plausible hardening. I verified the code doesn't check the
  resulting mode. Mitigated: two real processes started at the same moment
  passed, and WAL persists after the first creation. Starting a new instance
  under live load wasn't tested. A cheap fix is to check the mode after the
  switch and fail loudly.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: 3
  Finding: Request size and supplier IDs: the body has no size cap,
  unattributable requests are never throttled, and every distinct supplier ID
  gets its own limit and its own permanent stats row (and variants like "acme "
   or "ACME" are separate suppliers).
  My verdict: Known limitations, not new. You decided supplier IDs compare
  exactly, there's no auth, and the brief says no hardening is expected. One
  genuinely new detail: stats rows for fake supplier IDs are never cleaned.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: 4
  Finding: Blocking calls: the ingest work is synchronous inside an async
  action,
  so a burst can hold thread-pool threads for up to the 15-second lock wait.
  My verdict: Known and measured, as covered in the locking measurements.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: 5
  Finding: Cleanup service: it blocks a pool thread while waiting for the lock,
  and shutdown isn't observed until the current pass ends.
  My verdict: Minor. Same root cause as #4.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: 6
  Finding: Duplicate with a newer version still writes the new version, which
  seems to contradict "don't write it again."
  My verdict: By design, from your decision in the challenge round, and
  explained
  in a code comment. Business fields are never rewritten. The README doesn't
  mention it, so a reviewer may ask the same question.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: 7
  Finding: Timestamps are truncated to milliseconds.
  My verdict: Negligible. The spec's timestamps are whole seconds.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: 8
  Finding: DB clock needs SQLite 3.42+ and truncates by up to 1 ms.
  My verdict: Not applicable. The app uses its bundled SQLite (3.53.3, verified
  in the spike), not a system one. A 1 ms truncation is harmless.

  Limits of this review

  - Single pass: it took about 45 seconds and six tool calls, and a model
    reading the code isn't exhaustive.
  - No tests run: it didn't run any, and it didn't know our earlier decisions,
    which is why #3 to #6 are reported as problems.

  My recommendation

  - Fix #1 and #2: both are small, I'd do them test-first, and the fixes fit in
    one commit. For #1 the test would force the rollback to fail and check that
    the original error is still logged and handled.
  - Leave #3 to #8 as they are. #6 is deliberate and #7 and #8 don't apply.
  - Optional README line: the README no longer mentions these limitations since
    plan.md was deleted. A one-line "known limitations" note would let a
    reviewer see they were considered. That's your call, given the length limit.

  Do you want me to fix #1 and #2? And should I add a one-line limitations note
  to the README?

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

● Findings #1 and #2 are fixed, and the whole suite passes at 188 tests, up from
  186. Nothing is committed yet.

  Fix 1: the failure handler

  - Reproduced first: a database trigger using RAISE(ROLLBACK) aborts the whole
    transaction, the way a full disk would. The rollback then throws This 
    SqliteTransaction has completed; it is no longer usable. Before the fix the
    request died unhandled and the original error was never logged.
  - The fix: the original error is logged before any rollback is attempted. If
    the rollback itself fails, that is logged as a second error. The request
    then returns a clean failed result, and the commit is skipped since there's
    nothing left to commit.
  - What can't be kept: in that rare case, even the request's quota slot is
    gone, because the transaction no longer exists. The test checks this, and
    checks that the next request works normally.

  Fix 2: startup journal mode

  - Reproduced first: SQLite raises no error when it can't switch to WAL. It
    just reports the mode it's still in. The initializer ignored that answer. An
    in-memory database starts fine but can never use WAL, and before the fix it
    started silently.
  - The fix: the initializer checks the answer and throws a clear error at
    startup, naming the mode it actually got. The error message says the
    throttle relies on WAL so that readers never block the single writer.

  Tests and checks

  - Two new tests, one per fix.
  - A wrong first attempt: my first version of the WAL test used a read-only
    file. It failed earlier, in the schema step, so it wasn't testing the WAL
    check. I caught that on the red run and switched to the in-memory approach.
  - Mutation check: four deliberate breaks were each caught by the matching
    test. Breaking the rollback handling, committing on a dead transaction and
    not logging the original error each fail the first test. Skipping the mode
    check fails the second.

  What I left alone

  I didn't touch the other seven findings. The working tree has these changes
  uncommitted: 2 source files and 2 test files for the fixes, plus your own
  README edits. Do you want me to commit and push only the fix files and leave
  your README edits out? That would keep your in-progress README text separate.

  One reminder: the /export you ran was cancelled, so the transcript still needs
  exporting at the end.
